A transfer principle for branched rough paths
F.Emilio Ferrucci

Abstract
A branched rough path 𝑿 \boldsymbol{X} consists of a rough integral calculus for X : [ 0 , T ] → R d X\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb R^{d} which may fail to satisfy integration by parts. Using Kelly’s bracket extension [ 30 ] , we define a notion of pushforward of branched rough paths through smooth maps, which leads naturally to a definition of branched rough path on a smooth manifold. Once a covariant derivative is fixed, we are able to give a canonical, coordinate-free definition of integral against such rough paths. After characterising quasi-geometric rough paths in terms of their bracket extension, we use the same framework to define manifold-valued rough differential equations (RDEs) driven by quasi-geometric rough paths valued in a different manifold. These results extend previous work on 3 > p 3>p -rough paths [ 1 ] , itself a generalisation of the Itô calculus on manifolds developed by Schwartz, Meyer and Émery [ 42 , 37 , 13 , 14 ] , to the setting of non-geometric rough calculus of arbitrarily low regularity.



keywords
[class=MSC]
Primary 60L20
, 60L70
; secondary 16T05

keywords

branched rough paths
, manifolds

Mathematical Institute, University of Oxford, United Kingdom, ??

Introduction

The theory of rough paths, introduced by Lyons
[
35
]
, consists of a framework for giving rigorous mathematical meaning to controlled differential equations
\mathrm{d}Y=V(Y)\mathrm{d}X
governed by an input signal
X
of low path regularity. This theory is based on the idea that, once certain additional data
\boldsymbol{X}
— a
rough path
— is provided, the solution
Y
can be made sense of in terms of a Taylor-type expansion, in a manner that makes the mapping
\boldsymbol{X}\mapsto Y
continuous under certain
p
-variation norms. The role of
\boldsymbol{X}
is to emulate the first few iterated integrals of
X
on the simplex — the exact number required depending on the regularity of
X
— in a precise algebraic and analytic sense. If
X
is a stochastic process, its enhancement
\boldsymbol{X}
can often (non-uniquely) be defined through probabilistic notions of convergence, although abstract existence theorems have also been proved
[
34
]
. Rough path theory has been shown to agree with the stochastic calculus of semimartingales (see
[
21
,
17
]
for a detailed account), but also to extend to novel settings, most notably that of Gaussian processes
[
11
,
20
]
.

The rough paths that are considered most often are
geometric
ones, i.e. those which satisfy a generalised integration by parts identity, expressed through the use of the shuffle product. Because such integration theories also satisfy the same change of variables formulae seen in ordinary multivariable calculus, it is not difficult to make the theory coordinate-free, and thus to define it in the setting of smooth manifolds, despite the fact that the signal
X
may still be arbitrarily irregular in the sense of
p
-variation. This principle was first observed in relation to Stratonovich calculus (prior to the introduction of rough paths), and is what Malliavin called the
transfer principle
: “many classical geometric constructions involving smooth curves extend to semimartingales via Stratonovich integrals”
[
13
, p.94]
. It is the reason that stochastic differential geometry is formulated almost exclusively in terms of Stratonovich, not Itô, integration (e.g.
[
29
]
).

It is, in fact, possible to give an intrinsic formulation of Itô calculus on manifolds, despite it not being geometric in the rough path sense. This was first discovered by Schwartz
[
42
]
, who relied on second-order vectors and forms, and Meyer
[
37
,
38
]
, who realised that a connection could be used to project these down onto the ordinary (co)tangent bundle. Émery further clarified these ideas
[
13
]
, naming them the
Itô transfer principle

[
14
]
: its main distinction with the smooth or Stratonovich calculi is that it depends on a covariant derivative on the manifold. In a nutshell, the idea is to replace the coordinate Itô differential
\mathrm{d}X^{\gamma}
with a modified version of it

\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}X^{\gamma}=\mathrm{d}X^{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}(X)\mathrm{d}[X]^{\alpha\beta}

(1)

where
\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
are the Christoffel symbols of the connection and
[X]^{\alpha\beta}
is the quadratic variation in the chosen coordinate system. The benefit of this substitution is that (
1
) now transforms as a vector, and may therefore be integrated against invariantly. The property of
\int\!\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}X
being a real-valued local martingale is also invariant under change of coordinates. Because of the second-order nature of the Itô lemma, these properties do not hold if one neglects the term
\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}(X)\mathrm{d}[X]^{\alpha\beta}
. In
[
1
]
the Itô transfer principle is extended to general
3>p
-rough paths, further elaborating on the extrinsic theory and on general notions of parallel transport and Cartan development. The regularity constraint allows one to recover the semimartingale case, as well as that of other processes, such as
1/3<H
-fractional Brownian motion (see
[
41
]
for an example of a non-geometric rough path for such process).

The question of the correct setting for a calculus of non-geometric rough paths, raised in
[
35
, p.273]
, was answered by Gubinelli in
[
24
]
for the finite-dimensional case (see
[
12
,
44
]
for the infinite-dimensional case). The idea is to no longer consider the rough path only to be constituted of objects representing linear iterated integrals, but also products of integrals of products of integrals of products… and so on. Such quantities are naturally indexed by non-planar rooted trees: in algebraic terms, this amounts to defining
\boldsymbol{X}
not as a functional on the shuffle Hopf algbera, but on the Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra. The benefit of considering such an indexing is that, while the shuffle identity constrains the product of two iterated integrals in the geometric case, no assumptions on integration by parts identities need to be made in the ramified case. The theory of
branched rough paths
was further elaborated on in
[
26
,
30
]
, in the latter of which the generalisation of Itô’s lemma, valid at arbitrarily low orders of regularity, is proved. A particularly interesting fact which is observed (and not predictable from the Itô case) is the following: the change of variable formula needed for solutions of rough differential equations (RDEs) driven by a branched rough path
\boldsymbol{X}
is strictly more complex than that which is used to only transform
X
itself. More recently, branched rough paths have been investigated in settings different to the Euclidean one:
[
9
]
studies the case in which the environment is a homogeneous space.

The purpose of this article is to define integration and differential equations for non-geometric rough paths of low regularity on manifolds. In this sense, we are generalising the Émery-Itô transfer principle to arbitrarily rough and algebraically ill-behaved integration theories. In order to state our main results, it will be necessary to establish some algebraic and combinatorial concepts relating to branched rough paths, generalising previous work on geometric rough paths
[
8
]
. Of chief importance will be the notion of pushforward of a rough path through a smooth map, which is used to make sense of branched rough paths on manifolds: while in the geometric case this operation is performed through the use of the ordered shuffle
[
32
]
, in the ramified case it will involve an operation on forests that consists of growing new trees out of existing vertices. When pushing forward a branched rough path, it will be necessary to also push forward the (simple) bracket extension: this can be viewed as a generalisation of the rule for obtaining the quadratic covariation of Itô integrals, and requires an additional consistency relation on the simple bracket. Because of the aforementioned additional complexity in the transformation rule for RDE solutions, our transfer principle will not apply to general RDEs on manifolds. Because of this, we will focus on the case of rough paths that can be defined on Hoffman’s quasi-shuffle algebra
[
27
]
.
Quasi-geometric
rough paths
[
5
]
lie somewhere between the geometric and the most general branched ones, and capture many cases of interest, including Itô calculus. In order to achieve the goal of writing RDEs on manifolds driven by quasi-geometric rough paths in a coordinate-free fashion, we will characterise them as those branched ones
\boldsymbol{X}
whose RDE solutions transform in the same way as
X
does: this result, which we believe to be of independent interest, is what allows us to employ the same transfer principle used for rough integrals in the general branched case, for RDEs in the quasi-geometric case. In our two main results
Theorem 4.3
and
Theorem 5.2
we establish the canonical formulae in local coordinates for integrals against branched rough paths on manifolds and for RDEs on manifolds driven by a quasi-geometric rough path (possibly valued in a second manifold). These are computable for arbitrarily rough
\boldsymbol{X}
, but rapidly increase in complexity; in (
105
) and (
111
) we write them down explicitly for rough paths of finite
4>p
-variation (
3\leq p<4
being the first case not already covered in the literature). The core of the proof of these results, contained in
Proposition 4.2
, involves defining certain coefficients as smooth functions of the Christoffel symbols of the connection, and exploiting certain cancellations of high-order partial derivatives of the change of coordinates.

This article is structured as follows. In
section 1
we review the background on branched rough paths and their bracket extensions. In
section 2
we define the lift of a controlled path, a special case of which is the pushforward. The aim of
section 3
is to discuss and characterise quasi-geometric rough paths. In
section 4
we define the transfer principle necessary to define rough integrals of one-forms against branched rough paths on manifolds, and in
section 5
we use the same transfer principle to study quasi-geometric RDEs on manifolds. Finally, in
6
Conclusions and further directions
we end by discussing a few interesting related problems that could be tackled in future work.

Acknowledgements.

I would like to thank Thomas Cass, Damiano Brigo and John Armstrong for their supervision of my graduate studies, and for the many helpful discussions that I had with them on these and many other related topics. Moreover, I am grateful to Michel Émery for introducing me to Itô calculus on manifolds, which constitutes the primary motivation for this article, when he visited Imperial College in 2016. Finally, I would like to acknowledge the many helpful remarks of the two anonymous reviewers, which I believe have improved the quality of this manuscript.

1
Background on
\mathbb R^{d}
-valued branched rough paths

1.1
The Connes-Kreimer and Grossman-Larson Hopf algebras

In this subsection we will go over the algebra necessary for the rest of the paper. We will follow mainly
[
19
]
(see also
[
28
]
) for Hopf algebras of forests and
[
40
,
36
]
for the more general theory of Hopf algebras; when details and proofs are omitted it is intended that they are to be found therein. Most of the choices in setup and notations will follow
[
26
]
(for instance in the decision to define the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra using forests rather than trees with unlabelled root, as done in many other parts of the literature), but will deviate from it in some aspects that will be motivated later on (for instance in the use of inhomogeneous gradings, used in
[
43
]
).

We will be interested in
A
-
decorated non-planar rooted forests
, where
A
is a finite alphabet. These are finite acyclic graphs with a finite number of vertices, which are labelled with elements of
A
, and each connected component of which - a
tree
- has a preferred vertex, its
root
. In graphical representations the root of a tree will be identified as its single lowermost vertex. The term “non-planar” refers to the fact that the trees in a forest, and the
children
of each vertex (the vertices attached to it that are further away from the root) are not given an order. An example is

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

in general for
a,\ldots,g\in A
. The first and second terms are equal by non-planarity, and the third is different (unless
e=f
). Trees such as this term - ones in which each vertex has at most one child - are called
ladders
.

Call the set of such forests
\mathcal{F}^{A}
and its subset of trees
\mathcal{T}^{A}
; note that we are considering an empty forest
\varnothing\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
(which is not considered a tree, as it has no root). We will say that a non-empty forest is
proper
if it is not a tree. We will denote forests using letters
{f},{g},{h},\ldots
and trees with
{r},{s},{t},\ldots
We will write
\nu\in{f}
to mean that
\nu
is a vertex of
{f}
and we denote
\ell(\nu)
its label. We will assume that
A
comes with a
weighting
, i.e. each element
a\in A
has a weight
|a|\in\mathbb N^{*}
. This induces a grading on
\mathcal{F}^{A}
by setting
|{f}|\coloneqq\sum_{\nu\in{f}}|\ell(\nu)|
, the
degree
of
{f}
. We will instead denote
\#{f}
the number of vertices of
{f}
; more in general, we will use
\#
to denote cardinality, reserving
|\cdot|
for weightings and gradings. It will sometimes be helpful to write
{f}={t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n}
when the forest
{f}
is composed of the individual trees
{t}_{1},\ldots,{t}_{n}
, which are called its
factors
; note that this product, which will also be defined between forests and denoted simply
{}\cdot{}
, is the free abelian one. It will also be helpful to use the notation
{t}=[{f}]_{a}
when the tree
{t}
is given by joining each root in the forest
{f}
to a new root labelled
a\in A
, and note that
[\varnothing]_{a}\coloneqq\hbox to11.61pt{\vbox to10.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
.

An important case for the alphabet is

[d]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,d\}

(2)

for some
d\in\mathbb N^{*}
, with the homogeneous grading
|\gamma|\equiv 1
for
\gamma=1,\ldots,d
. In this case we will denote
\mathcal{F}^{A}\eqqcolon\mathcal{F}^{d}
, and similarly replace all
A
superscripts with
d
’s. We will usually use Greek letters for elements of
[d]
, reserving
a,b,c,\ldots
for more general labels.

We will now introduce algebraic operations on
\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle
, the graded
\mathbb R
-vector space generated by
\mathcal{F}^{A}
, of which we identify the subspace generated by the empty forest with
\mathbb R
, by
\varnothing=1
. A non-total
cut

C
of
{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}
is a subset of its edges. It is called
admissible
if it has the property that every increasing path in
{t}
contains at most one element of
C
. For example

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

both define cuts of the underlying tree, but only the first is admissible. The admissible cut
\varnothing
is called the
trivial cut
. Deleting the edges in a non-total cut
C
transforms
{t}
into a forest
{t}_{C}
; if
C
is admissible we call
\overline{{t}}_{C}
the tree containing its root (think of the portion of
{t}
below the cut) and
\underline{{t}}_{C}
the forest comprised of all other factors of
{f}
(think of the portion of
{t}
above the cut). The trivial cut can be thought of as a cut above the leaves, since
\overline{{t}}_{\varnothing}={t}
,
\underline{{t}}_{\varnothing}=\varnothing
. We also consider the
total cut

\forall
, which is declared admissible and for which we set
\overline{{t}}_{\forall}=\varnothing
,
\underline{{t}}_{\forall}={t}
; this cut, which does not correspond to any set of edges, should be thought of as a cut below the root. The set of cuts of
{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}
(including
\forall
) is denoted
\mathrm{Cut}({t})
and its subset of admissible ones
\mathrm{Cut}^{*}({t})
. We will also speak of cuts of a forest
{t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n}
: this is just a collection of cuts, one for each
{t}_{k}
. The
Connes-Kreimer coproduct
is given by

\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}\colon\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\to\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\otimes\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle,\quad\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}{t}\coloneqq\sum_{C\in\mathrm{Cut}^{*}({t})}\underline{{t}}_{C}\otimes\overline{{t}}_{C}\ \text{ for }{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}

(3)

and required to be an algebra morphism according to the free abelian product of trees, i.e.
\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}({t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n})=\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}{t}_{1}\cdots\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}{t}_{n}
with the product on the right given factor-wise, and extending linearly. For example

\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}\hbox to27.92pt{\vbox to30.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.71935pt\lower-4.83995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\otimes 1.

We now define the operation that is dual to
\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}
, in a sense that will be made precise below. For
{t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n}={f},{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
we will say that
{h}
is obtained by
grafting

{f}
onto
{g}
, denoted
{h}\in{f}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{g}
, if
{h}
is obtained by taking each factor
{t}_{k}
and either joining its root to a vertex of
{g}
(by adding an extra edge) or multiplying it with
{g}
(i.e. making it one of the factors of
{h}
). Note that when we sum over
{h}\in{f}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{g}
we are not doing so over all distinct forests that are given by grafting
{f}
onto
{g}
, but over distinct ways of grafting: the point is that there may be two distinct vertices in
{g}
s.t. grafting
{f}
onto them results in two identical labelled forests; for this reason
{f}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{g}
is best thought as a multiset, not a set. Also note that
\varnothing\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{g}
and
{g}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\varnothing
both consist of the singleton
\{{g}\}
. We then define the
Grossman-Larson product

\star\colon\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\otimes\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\to\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle,\quad{f}\star{g}\coloneqq\sum_{{h}\in{f}\hbox to8.08pt{\vbox to2.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.3379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 1.57 L 10.63 -1.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{g}}{h}

(4)

and extending linearly. An example is

Note how the last two summands are the same if
b=c
. There is one operation that is left to define: the coproduct dual to the free abelian product of forests. We define the
Grossman-Larson coproduct

\Delta_{\mathrm{GL}}\colon\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\to\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\otimes\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle,\quad\Delta_{\mathrm{GL}}({t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n})\coloneqq\sum_{I\sqcup J=\{1,\ldots,n\}}{t}_{I}\otimes{t}_{J}

(5)

where we are summing over all subsets
I
of the set with
n
elements, with
J
its complement, and for
K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
we are defining
{t}_{K}\coloneqq\prod_{k\in K}{t}_{k}
. We now define the algebraic structure into which we would like these operations to fit.

Definition 1.1

(Connected graded bialgebra)
.

A
connected graded bialgebra
is a triple
(H,\times,\Delta)
where
H=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}H^{n}
is a graded real vector space,
\times\colon H\otimes H\to H
(the
product
) and
\Delta\colon H\to H\otimes H
(the
coproduct
) are linear functions, and the following axioms are satisfied for all
x,y,z\in H
:

Associativity.

(x\times y)\times z=x\times(y\times z)
;

Unit.

There exists a
unit
, i.e. a linear map
\iota\colon\mathbb R\to H
s.t.
x\times\iota(1)=x=\iota(1)\times x
and
\Delta\circ\iota=\iota\otimes\iota
;

Coassociativity.

(\mathbbm 1_{H}\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta=(\Delta\otimes\mathbbm 1_{H})\circ\Delta
;

Counit.

There exists a
counit
, i.e. a linear map
\epsilon\colon H\to\mathbb R
s.t.

(\epsilon\otimes\mathbbm 1_{H})\circ\Delta=\mathbbm 1_{H}=(\mathbbm 1_{H}\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta
and
\epsilon(x\times y)=\epsilon x\times\epsilon y
;

Compatibility.

\Delta(x\times y)=\Delta x\times\Delta y
and
\epsilon\circ\iota=\mathbbm 1R
;

Grading.

H^{i}\times H^{j}\subseteq H^{i+j}
and
\Delta H^{n}\subseteq\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H^{i}\otimes H^{j}
;

Connectedness.

H^{0}\cong\mathbb R
.

From these axioms, which define a connected graded bialgebra, it is possible to show the following further property, that (uniquely) turns
H
into a Hopf algebra:

Antipode.

There exists a unique
antipode
, i.e. a linear map
\mathcal{S}\colon H\to H
s.t.

\times\circ(\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathbbm 1_{H})\circ\Delta=\iota\circ\epsilon=\times\circ(\mathbbm 1_{H}\otimes\mathcal{S})\circ\Delta
.

We also consider the following two optional properties, denoting
\tau\colon H\otimes H\to H\otimes H
the switch of factors:

Commutativity

\times\circ\tau=\times
;

Cocommutativity

\tau\circ\Delta=\Delta
.

If the unit and co-unit exist they are unique, and moreover we have
\operatorname{Im}\iota=H^{0}
,
\operatorname{Ker}\epsilon=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H^{n}
, and we will use
\iota
to identify
H^{0}=\mathbb R
. Less trivially, uniqueness also holds for the antipode: this fact, the proof of which uses the grading in an essential way, means that the antipode does not constitute additional structure; in a similar spirit, it can be shown that a bialgebra morphism, defined in the obvious way, automatically preserves the antipode. The
reduced coproduct
is defined by

\widetilde{\Delta}x\coloneqq\Delta x-x\otimes 1-1\otimes x\in\bigoplus_{i,j\geq 1}H^{i}\otimes H^{j}.

(6)

We may also iterate the coproduct by defining

\begin{split}&\Delta^{m}\colon H\to H^{\otimes m},\quad\Delta^{0}\coloneqq 1R,\ \Delta^{1}\coloneqq\mathbbm 1,\ \Delta^{2}\coloneqq\Delta,\\
&\Delta^{m}\coloneqq(\mathbbm 1_{H}\otimes\Delta^{m-1})\circ\Delta=(\Delta^{m-1}\otimes\mathbbm 1_{H})\circ\Delta\quad\text{for }m\geq 3\end{split}

(7)

with the last identity holding by coassociativity. We will use Sweedler notation

\Delta^{m}x\eqqcolon\sum_{(x)^{m}}x_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{(m)}

(8)

and we can modify the subscript
(x)^{m}
to reflect whether we are reducing the coproduct, i.e.
(\widetilde{x})^{m}
, and/or to specify the specific coproduct used, e.g.
(x)_{\mathrm{CK}}^{m}
,
(x)_{\mathrm{GL}}^{m}
, and the superscript
m
will be omitted when it is
2
.

An element
x\in H
is
primitive
if
\Delta x=1\otimes x+x\otimes 1
and
grouplike
if
\Delta x=x\otimes x
. The set of primitive elements will be denoted
\mathcal{P}(H)
and forms a Lie algebra with bracket
[x,y]\coloneqq x\times y-y\times x
, in which
\mathcal{S}x=-x
. The set of grouplike elements will be denoted
\mathcal{G}(H)
and forms a group in which
\mathcal{S}x=x^{-1}
. Such statements are not difficult to prove, e.g. for
x\in\mathcal{G}(H)

\mathcal{S}x\times x=\times\circ(\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathbbm 1_{H})(x\otimes x)=(\times\circ(\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathbbm 1_{H})\circ\Delta)x=1.

We will adopt contractions in notation with obvious meaning such as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{GL}}^{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})
(with
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
defined below).

Definition 1.2

(The Connes-Kreimer and Grossman-Larson Hopf algebras)
.

We call the triple
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}\coloneqq(\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle,\cdot,\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}})
(where
\cdot
denotes the free abelian product of forests) the
Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra
, and the triple
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}\coloneqq(\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle,\star,\Delta_{\mathrm{GL}})
the
Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra
.

That
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
are bialgebras is non-trivial. Before stating this result, we define the pairing that establishes
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
as dual to one another. For
{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
define
\mathcal{N}({f})
to be the number of label-preserving order automorphisms of
{f}
: this is recursively given by

\mathcal{N}(\varnothing)=1,\qquad\mathcal{N}([{f}]_{a})=\mathcal{N}({f}),\qquad\mathcal{N}({s}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{s}_{m}^{k_{m}})=\prod_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}!\mathcal{N}({s}_{i})^{k_{i}}

(9)

where
{s}_{1}\cdots{s}_{m}
are pairwise distinct trees when taking the labelling into account. We define the pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}\to\mathbb R,\quad\langle{f},{g}\rangle\coloneqq\mathcal{N}({f})\text{\textdelta}_{{f}{g}}

(10)

where
δ
denotes Kronecker delta, and we are extending with bilinearity. This, in turn, induces a pairing on
\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle^{\otimes n}
. The purpose of this pairing is for
\star
to be dual to
\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}
and
\cdot
to
\Delta_{\mathrm{GL}}
with respect to it. The reason for the
\mathcal{N}({f})
factor is explained by the following example: calling
\text{\textdelta}(\cdot,\cdot)
the pairing induced by the Kronecker delta on basis elements, we have

\text{\textdelta}\big(\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}\!\!\hbox to22.38pt{\vbox to25.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.19229pt\lower-5.76355pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

It can be observed that something similar thing occurs with the operations
\cdot
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{GL}}
. We now state the result that summarises the content of this subsection:

Theorem 1.3

(The pair
(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}},\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})
)
.

\mathcal{H}_{\emph{CK}}^{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\emph{GL}}^{A}
are connected graded Hopf algebras, the former commutative and the latter cocommutative, and the map (
10
) defines a graded bialgebra pairing, i.e.

\langle\Delta_{\emph{CK}}z,x\otimes y\rangle=\langle z,x\star y\rangle,\qquad\langle x\otimes y,\Delta_{\emph{GL}}z\rangle=\langle xy,z\rangle

(11)

and

\begin{split}i\neq j\Rightarrow\langle(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\emph{CK}})_{i},(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\emph{GL}})^{j}\rangle=0,\quad\langle x,(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\emph{GL}})^{|x|}\rangle=0\Rightarrow x=0,\quad\langle(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\emph{CK}})_{|y|},y\rangle=0\Rightarrow y=0.\end{split}

(12)

Note how the above notion of graded duality is different from ordinary duality: the former is equivalent to an isomorphism
\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}H^{n}\cong\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}H_{n}
, where
H_{n}
is the dual
H^{n}
, defined unambiguously if
H_{n}
is finite-dimensional, as is the case here. Graded duality has the advantage of not introducing direct products (which would be needed in the study of full signatures, but are not when only dealing with rough paths), while still maintaining a lot of the necessary functoriality. For example, if
f\colon\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}H^{n}\to\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}K^{n}
is a linear map for which there exists
m
s.t.
f(H_{n})\subseteq K_{n+m}
for all
n
, it induces a unique map
f^{*}\colon\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}K_{n}\to\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb N}H_{n}
s.t.
\langle f^{*}(y),x\rangle=\langle y,f(x)\rangle
.

The (uniquely determined) Connes-Kreimer antipode is given by

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CK}}({t})=\sum_{\forall\neq C\in\mathrm{Cut}({t})}(-1)^{|C|+1}{t}_{C},\quad{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}

(13)

(note that we are summing over all non-total cuts, not just the admissible ones) and extended as an algebra morphism. In a graded dual pair of Hopf algebras, the antipodes are graded dual to one another, so we can obtain the Grossman-Larson antipode as
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{GL}}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CK}}^{*}
.

Note that the fact that the pairing of
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
is not the Kronecker one implies that covariant and contravariant components no longer coincide: we reserve sub/super-scripting for the former, i.e. we denote

x^{f}\coloneqq\langle f,x\rangle,\quad y_{f}\coloneqq\langle y,f\rangle\qquad\text{for }x\in\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}},\ y\in\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}},\ {f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}.

(14)

The contravariant component of
x
w.r.t.
{f}
, on the other hand, is
\text{\textdelta}({f},x)
, meaning that we can express
x
as the finite sum
\sum_{{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\text{\textdelta}({f},x){f}
(and similarly for
y\in\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
). As a consequence
\langle y,x\rangle\neq y_{f}x^{f}
, rather

\begin{split}\langle y,x\rangle&=\Big\langle\sum_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\text{\textdelta}(y,g)g,\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\text{\textdelta}(x,f)f\Big\rangle\\
&=\Big\langle\sum_{g\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\mathcal{N}(g)^{-1}\langle g,y\rangle g,\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\mathcal{N}(f)^{-1}\langle f,x\rangle f\Big\rangle\\
&=\sum_{f,g\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\mathcal{N}(g)^{-1}\mathcal{N}(f)^{-1}y_{g}x^{f}\langle g,f\rangle\\
&=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\mathcal{N}(f)^{-1}y_{f}x^{f}.\end{split}

(15)

We conclude this subsection with a remark.

Remark 1.4

(Forest bialgebras over abstract vector spaces)
.

\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
may be considered “bialgebras over
\mathbb R^{A}
”, in the sense that
(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}})_{1}=\mathbb R^{A}=(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})^{1}
canonically. The theory needed to replace
\mathbb R^{A}
with a possibly infinite-dimensional (locally-convex) abstract vector space is developed in
[
44
]
. Since we confine ourselves to the finite-dimensional case, for our purposes it makes more sense to begin by fixing coordinates, and later make the manifold-valued theory coordinate-free by considering suitably compatible families of rough paths defined w.r.t. arbitrary charts.

1.2
Rough paths, their controlled paths, rough integration and RDEs

In this subsection we introduce the topic of branched rough paths, original to
[
24
]
. We will follow
[
26
]
, and omit proofs and details that can be found therein. We preface the main definition with a couple of preliminary ones. For
T\geq 0
let
\Delta_{T}\coloneqq\{(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}\mid s\leq t\}
. A
control
on
[0,T]
is a continuous function
\omega\colon\Delta_{T}\to[0,+\infty)
s.t.
\omega(t,t)=0
for
0\leq t\leq T
and is superadditive, i.e.
\omega(s,u)+\omega(u,t)\leq\omega(s,t)
for
0\leq s\leq u\leq t\leq T
. Throughout this article,
p
will denote a real number
\in[1,+\infty)
. We will denote
(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})^{n}
the sub-vector space of
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
generated by forest of weight
n
and
(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})^{\leq n}
that generated by those of weight
k\leq n
. We will also use similar sub/superscripts for projection on such subspaces, e.g.
x^{\leq n}
is the projection of
x\in\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}}
onto
(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})^{\leq n}
. When referring to
\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
we will use subscripts instead of superscripts, to emphasize duality.

Definition 1.5

(Branched rough path)
.

An
\mathbb R^{A}
-valued
p
-
branched rough path
(of
inhomogeneous regularity
given by the weighting on
A
) on
[0,T]
controlled by
\omega
is a continuous map

\boldsymbol{X}\colon\Delta_{T}\to(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}},\quad(s,t)\mapsto\boldsymbol{X}_{st}

(16)

s.t.
\boldsymbol{X}^{\varnothing}\equiv 1
and satisfying the following three axioms:

Regularity.

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s<t\leq T}\frac{|\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{f}|}{\omega(s,t)^{|{f}|/p}}<\infty
for
{f}\in(\mathcal{F}^{A})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}}
;

Multiplicativity.

\boldsymbol{X}_{st}=(\boldsymbol{X}_{su}\star\boldsymbol{X}_{ut})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}}
, or in coordinates
\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{st}=\displaystyle\sum_{({f})_{\mathrm{CK}}}\boldsymbol{X}_{su}^{{f}_{(1)}}\boldsymbol{X}_{ut}^{{f}_{(2)}}
for
{f}\in(\mathcal{F}^{d})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}}
, and
0\leq s\leq u\leq t\leq T
;

Products.

\Delta_{\mathrm{GL}}\boldsymbol{X}_{st}=(\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{st})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}}
, or in coordinates
\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{{f}{g}}=\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{st}\boldsymbol{X}^{g}_{st}
for
{f},{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
with
|{f}|+|{g}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}
, and
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.

We denote the set of these
C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
.

The intuitive meaning of a branched rough path is given by the following recursive set of identities:

\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{\varnothing}=1,\qquad\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{{t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{t_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{t_{n}},\qquad\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{[f]_{a}}=\int_{s}^{t}\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{su}\mathrm{d}X_{u}^{a}

(17)

for
{t}_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{A},{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
. While the second identity is implied by the definition (and the first is actually required), the third is only to be taken heuristically, as the integral is not well defined in general. Of course, when
|{f}|\geq{\lfloor p\rfloor}
the term
\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{[{f}]_{a}}
could be defined by taking the above identity literally in the sense of Young: this, together with the identity for products, would automatically define
\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{g}
for any
{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
and is called the
Lyons extension
of
\boldsymbol{X}
; in this paper, however, we will always consider
\boldsymbol{X}
to be truncated at order
{\lfloor p\rfloor}
: this will ensure that all of our sums are finite, yet precise at the necessary order. When equipped with an initial value
X_{0}
, the components of
\boldsymbol{X}
indexed by single labelled vertices are the increments of components
X^{a}
of a continuous function
X\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb R^{A}
called the
trace
;
X
is a member of
\mathcal{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
, the set of functions
Y\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb R^{A}
with the property that for
a\in A

\sup_{0\leq s<t\leq T}\frac{|Y^{a}_{st}|}{\omega(s,t)^{|a|/p}}

(18)

where
Y_{st}\coloneqq Y_{t}-Y_{s}
is the increment. Note that the definitions of
C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
and
\mathcal{C}
depend on the weights assigned to elements of
A
. Also note that our setup accommodates rough paths of inhomogeneous
\omega
-Hölder regularity, but only ones that are integer multiples of a given
p^{-1}
: this choice is justified by the fact that the bracket terms considered in
subsection 1.3
(the only reason that has prompted us to consider inhomogeneous regularities) satisfy this property.

In what follows we will write
\approx_{m}
between two real-valued quantities dependent on
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
to mean that their difference lies in
O(\omega(s,t)^{m/p})
as
t\searrow s
, and simply
\approx
(
almost
equal) to mean
\approx_{{\lfloor p\rfloor}+1}
. We now give the definition of path controlled by a branched rough path.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
. An
\mathbb R^{e}
-valued
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled path
\boldsymbol{H}
is an element of
\mathcal{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],({\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}}^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}-1})^{\times e})
with homogeneous grading
\equiv 1
on the target, and s.t. for
n=0,\ldots,{\lfloor p\rfloor}-2
(and automatically satisfied by regularity of
\boldsymbol{H}
for
n={\lfloor p\rfloor}-1
)

\mathbb R^{e}\ni\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{t},y\rangle\approx_{{\lfloor p\rfloor}-n}\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{s},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\star y\rangle,\quad y\in(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{GL}})^{n}.

(19)

Call the set of these
D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e})
.

Note that
\boldsymbol{H}
is a vector space-valued path; the homogeneous grading on the
e
-fold cartesian product of
(\mathcal{H}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}-1}
just reflects the fact that its components, indexed by
[e]\times(\mathcal{F}^{A})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}-1}
(recall that we are denoting
[e]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,e\}
), all have regularity
p
: this is because in all cases of interest
\boldsymbol{H}
will be defined explicitly in terms of the whole of
\boldsymbol{X}
and will therefore be, in general, as regular as the least regular component of
X
. We will denote the components of
\boldsymbol{H}
as
\boldsymbol{H}^{k}_{f}
where
{f}\in(\mathcal{F}^{A})^{\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}-1}
and
k=1,\ldots,e
; the terms
H\coloneqq\boldsymbol{H}_{\varnothing}\in\mathcal{C}^{p}([0,T],\mathbb R^{e})
will be called the
trace
, and the rest its
Gubinelli derivatives
. Note that in (
19
) the pairing is intended as componentwise on the upper index, which we will often omit. Using (
15
) we can express (
19
) as

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{H}_{f;t}&\approx_{{\lfloor p\rfloor}-|{f}|}\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{s},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\star f\rangle\\
&=\langle\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}\boldsymbol{H}_{s},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\otimes{f}\rangle\\
&=\sum_{{g},{h}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\mathcal{N}({f})^{-1}\mathcal{N}({g})^{-1}\langle\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}\boldsymbol{H}_{s},{g}\otimes{h}\rangle\langle{g}\otimes{h},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\otimes{f}\rangle\\
&=\sum_{{g},{h}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}}\mathcal{N}({f})^{-1}\mathcal{N}({g})^{-1}\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{s},{g}\star{h}\rangle\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{g}\langle{h},{f}\rangle\\
&\approx_{{\lfloor p\rfloor}-|{f}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}\\
|{g}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}-|{f}|\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}({g})^{-1}\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{s},{g}\star{f}\rangle\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{g}\end{split}

or in other words

\boldsymbol{H}_{f;t}\approx_{{\lfloor p\rfloor}-|{f}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}\\
|{g}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}-|{f}|\\
{h}\in{g}\hbox to8.08pt{\vbox to2.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.3379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 1.57 L 10.63 -1.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{f}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}({g})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{h};s}\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{g}

(20)

which at the trace level reads

H_{t}\approx_{{\lfloor p\rfloor}}\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{s},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A},\ |{g}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}({g})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{g};s}\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{g}.

(21)

Example 1.7
.

To convince oneself that this is indeed the form of the expansion that is self-consistent at higher orders, consider the ODE

\mathrm{d}Y=V(Y)\mathrm{d}X,\quad V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{e},\mathbb R^{e\times d}).

(22)

Y
in terms of the branched iterated integrals of
X

and Taylor expansions of

Remark 1.8
.

As exemplified by the calculations above, sums of the sort
\sum_{{f}}\mathcal{N}({f})^{-1}\varphi({f})
can be replaced with ones
\sum_{\widetilde{}{f}}\sum_{\ell}\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{}{f})^{-1}\varphi(\widetilde{}{f}_{\ell})
, where
\widetilde{}{f}
are unlabelled forests (ranging in the set corresponding to that of the
{f}
’s), we are additionally summing over all possible labellings
\ell
on each
\widetilde{}{f}
(i.e. maps from the set of vertices of each
\widetilde{}{f}
to
A
),
\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{}{f})
is the number of unlabelled order automorphisms of
\widetilde{}{f}
, and
\widetilde{}{f}_{\ell}
is the forest
\widetilde{}{f}
labelled with
\ell
. This is because in the latter type of sum each term
\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{}{f})^{-1}\varphi({f})
appears
|\mathrm{Aut}(\widetilde{}{f})/\mathrm{Aut}({f})|=\mathcal{N}(\widetilde{}{f})/\mathcal{N}({f})
times.

Example 1.9

(Smooth functions of controlled paths)
.

If
\boldsymbol{H}\in D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e})
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{e},\mathbb R^{c})
we can define an
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled path with trace
f(H)
by

(f_{*}\boldsymbol{H})_{f}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{n})\in(\mathcal{F}^{A}\setminus\{\varnothing\})^{n}\\
{f}_{1}\cdots{f}_{n}={f}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{N}({f})}{n!\mathcal{N}({f}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({f}_{n})}\partial_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}f(H)\boldsymbol{H}^{k_{1}}_{{f}_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}^{k_{n}}_{{f}_{n}}

(23)

where we are summing on all distinct ways of expressing the forest
{f}
as a product of non-empty forests
{f}_{1}\cdots{f}_{n}
. In particular, if
e=d
and we take
\boldsymbol{H}
to be the
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled path defined by
X
itself, i.e.
\boldsymbol{X}^{a}_{{f}}=\text{\textdelta}^{\bullet^{a}}_{f}
, we have the expression for a controlled path given by a function of the trace of
X
:

\langle f(X),\hbox to15.51pt{\vbox to11.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{3.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\cdots\hbox to16.51pt{\vbox to11.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{3.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\rangle\coloneqq\partial_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}}f(X)

(24)

and zero on all other forests; in this case (
20
) reduces to the usual Taylor expansion. In this case we will often just write
f(X)
to denote the above controlled path, since it only depends on
f
and the trace
X
.

We continue by defining rough integration. We will call elements of
D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e\times A})

\boldsymbol{X}
-
controlled integrands
, and we will use subscripts for the
A
index, i.e.
\boldsymbol{H}^{k}_{{f},a}
for
k\in[e],\ a\in A,\ {f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
. Setting, for
a\in A

\langle{f},\boldsymbol{X}^{a}_{st}\rangle\coloneqq\langle[{f}]_{a},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\rangle,\quad{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}.

It is shown that

\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{a;s},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{a}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{a;s},\boldsymbol{X}_{su}^{a}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{a;u},\boldsymbol{X}_{ut}^{a}\rangle\approx 0

for
s\leq u\leq t
, enabling the following

Definition 1.10

(Rough integral)
.

We define the
rough integral
as the unique path with increments
\approx\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{a;s},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}^{a}\rangle
, i.e.

\int_{s}^{t}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{[u,v]\in\pi_{n}}\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{a;u},\boldsymbol{X}^{a}_{uv}\rangle

(25)

where
(\pi_{n})_{n}
is any sequence of partitions on
[s,t]
with vanishing step size as
n\to\infty
.

This limit is shown to be well defined, independently of
(\pi_{n})_{n}
, and taking
s=0,\ t\in[0,T]
above yields an element of
\mathcal{C}^{p}([0,T],\mathbb R^{e})
. In coordinates we have, by (
21
)

\int_{s}^{t}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A},\ a\in A\\
|{f}|+|a|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}({f})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{f},a;s}\boldsymbol{X}^{[{f}]_{a}}_{st}.

(26)

This becomes an element
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\in D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e})
by setting
(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X})_{\varnothing}
to the above path, and

\bigg(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\bigg)_{[{f}]_{a}}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{H}_{{f},a},\quad\bigg(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\bigg)_{{g}}\coloneqq 0,\quad\text{for }{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}\setminus(\mathcal{T}^{A}\cup\{\varnothing\}).

(27)

We move on to the topic of rough differential equations, or RDEs. Let
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{e},\mathbb R^{e\times d})
. We wish to give meaning to the expression

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Y}=F(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X},\quad Y_{0}=y_{0}\in\mathbb R^{e}.

(28)

Definition 1.11

(RDE)
.

We will say
\boldsymbol{Y}\in D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e})
is a
controlled solution
to (
28
) if

\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}=y_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}F_{*}\boldsymbol{Y}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}

(29)

where the
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled paths
\boldsymbol{Y}
and
F_{*}\boldsymbol{Y}
are defined by the rules (
27
) and (
23
).

Note that if such a
\boldsymbol{Y}
exists all its Gubinelli derivatives are automatically fixed by the trace
Y
and the smooth function
F
. Their expression can be computed more explicitly in terms of recursively-defined smooth functions of
Y
as

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{Y}_{[{f}]_{a};t}&=F_{[{f}]_{a}}\!(Y_{t}),\qquad\boldsymbol{Y}_{{g}}=0\quad\text{for }{g}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}\setminus(\mathcal{T}^{A}\cup\{\varnothing\})\\
\text{where}\quad F_{\varnothing}&\coloneqq\mathbbm 1_{\mathbb R^{e}},\quad F_{[{t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n}]_{a}}\coloneqq\partial_{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}F_{a}F^{k_{1}}_{{t}_{1}}\cdots F^{k_{n}}_{{t}_{n}}.\end{split}

(30)

We can use this and (
26
) to express the trace level of (
29
) as

Y_{st}\approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A},\ |{t}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}({t})^{-1}F_{{t}}\!(Y_{s})\boldsymbol{X}^{{t}}_{st},\quad Y_{0}=y_{0}.

(31)

This is known as the
Davie solution
, and it is equivalent to the notion of controlled solution in the sense that (
31
) holds if and only if there exists a controlled solution, which is necessarily given by (
30
). Another interesting feature of the coefficients
F_{t}
is how they behave when evaluated against Grossman-Larson products: it can be shown that

F_{({t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n})\star{s}}=\partial_{k_{1}\cdots k_{n}}F_{s}F^{k_{1}}_{{t}_{1}}\cdots F^{k_{1}}_{{t}_{n}},\qquad{t}_{1},\ldots,{t}_{n},{s}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}.

(32)

Taking
{s}=\hbox to10.85pt{\vbox to10.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{2.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
(and setting
F
to be zero on proper non-empty forests) reduces this identity to (
30
).

1.3
Kelly’s bracket extension

The lack of constraints on the product structure of branched rough paths results in rough integration against
\boldsymbol{X}
not being sufficiently rich to express increments of functions of
\boldsymbol{X}
-driven RDEs, not even of
X
itself. In this section we will review the material of
[
30
, Ch. 5]
, which remedies this lack of a change of variable formula by means of an ingenious procedure that consists of enlarging
\boldsymbol{X}
by recursively adding new trace components and coherently lifting to a rough path; details and proofs not included here are intended to be found therein.

Given the weighted alphabet
A
we consider the enlarged alphabet consisting of adding to
A
all non-trivial proper forests in
\mathcal{F}^{A}

\widehat{A}\coloneqq A\sqcup(\mathcal{F}^{A}\setminus(\mathcal{T}^{A}\sqcup\{\varnothing\}))

(33)

and its subalphabet consisting of commutative sequences, i.e. multisets, of letters in
A

\widetilde{A}\coloneqq\{(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})\mid n\in\mathbb N,\ a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A\}.

(34)

We are considering
A\subseteq\widetilde{A}\subseteq\widehat{A}
, with the second inclusion given by identifying
a_{1}\ldots a_{n}=\hbox to15.51pt{\vbox to11.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{3.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\cdots\hbox to16.51pt{\vbox to11.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{3.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!
. We denote the sets and Hopf algebras constructed w.r.t.
\widetilde{A},\widehat{A}
correspondingly, e.g.
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
. Elements of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{A}
are forests labelled with commutative products of elements of
A
, and elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{A}
are forests labelled with
A
and
A
-labelled non-trivial proper forests or single vertices. We will use round brackets to denote these new types of labels. The weighting on
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{A}
(and accordingly that on its subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{A}
) is just given by summing the weights of the labels as elements of
\mathcal{F}^{A}
, i.e. counting up the total number of elements in
A
.

Define the bilinear “root labelling” map

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}\colon\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\otimes\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\to\mathbb R\langle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{A}\rangle,\qquad{f}\otimes{g}\mapsto\begin{cases}0&{g}=\varnothing\vee({g}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}\wedge\#{g}>1)\\
[{f}]_{({g})}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{split}

(35)

and the
bracket polynomial
map

\ll\hskip-1.99997pt\cdot\hskip-1.99997pt\gg\coloneqq\mathbbm 1-\mathcal{J}\circ\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}\colon\mathbb R\langle\mathcal{F}^{A}\rangle\to\mathbb R\langle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}{}^{A}\rangle

(36)

where recall that
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{CK}}
denotes the reduced Connes-Kreimer coproduct. For products of single vertices (which we identify with their labels) this reduces to

\begin{split}&\ll\!\!c_{1}\ldots c_{n}\!\!\gg\coloneqq c_{1}\ldots c_{n}-\sum_{I\sqcup J=\{1,\ldots,n\}}[\hbox to15.51pt{\vbox to11.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{3.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}]_{(b_{1}\ldots b_{q})},\\
&I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\},\ J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}\},\quad a_{k}\coloneqq c_{i_{k}},\ b_{k}\coloneqq c_{j_{k}}.\end{split}

(37)

Definition 1.12

(Bracket extension)
.

A (
full
)
bracket extension

\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
of
\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
is a
p
-rough path over the alphabet
\widehat{A}
extending the existing one over
A
, and with the property that

\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({f})}=\langle\ll{f}\gg,\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle,\qquad{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}.

(38)

A
simple bracket extension
of
\boldsymbol{X}
is a
p
-rough path
\boldsymbol{X}
over the alphabet
\widetilde{A}
with the property that

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})}=\langle\ll\hskip-1.99997pta_{1}\ldots a_{n}\hskip-1.99997pt\gg,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle,\qquad a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A.

(39)

All of this is equivalent to expressing the evaluation of
\boldsymbol{X}
against a forest in terms of evaluations of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
against trees as

\boldsymbol{X}^{f}=\langle\mathcal{J}\circ\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}({f}),\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle

(40)

(with the simple case obtained by picking
{f}=\hbox to15.51pt{\vbox to11.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}

) where we are using the unreduced coproduct, with
[\varnothing]_{({g})}=\hbox to17.68pt{\vbox to14.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{3.783pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!
; note that this reduces to the trivial identity
\boldsymbol{X}^{t}=\boldsymbol{X}^{t}
when
{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}
, since the only term considered on the right hand side is the one corresponding to the cut that separates the root from everything else. A full bracket extension automatically defines a simple bracket extension by taking the trees in the forest
{f}
of (
38
) to be given by a product of single vertices.

In
[
30
, Proposition 5.3.9]
it is proved that for every branched rough path
\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
there exists a full bracket extension
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
of
\boldsymbol{X}
. A sketch of proof goes as follows. Beginning with the original branched rough path
\boldsymbol{X}
, recursively perform the following two steps:

•

Notice that the defining condition fixes the trace term
\widehat{X}^{({t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n})}
as being canonically determined by
\boldsymbol{X}^{{t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n}}=\boldsymbol{X}^{{t}_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{X}^{{t}_{n}}
(part of the original rough path) and lower degree rough path terms of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
. Also, show that the bracket polynomials
\ll{f}\gg
are primitive elements in
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{A}_{\mathrm{CK}}
, i.e.
\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}\ll{f}\gg=1\otimes\ll{f}\gg+\ll{f}\gg\otimes 1
, and that thus the bracket terms (
38
) are increments of paths:
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({f})}_{st}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({f})}_{su}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({f})}_{ut}
.

•

Consider the inductively-defined bracket extension over trace terms of degree at most
n
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(n)}
, together with the collection of bracket paths of degree
n+1
,
\widehat{X}^{(n+1)}
, defined in the previous step. Lift the pair
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(n)},\widehat{X}^{(n+1)}
to a rough path that agrees with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(n)}
and includes all necessary cross terms. This comes as a consequence of of a lemma,
[
30
, Corollary 4.2.16]
, which states that it is always possible to find a (branched) rough path lift of a path, compatibly with some of its (branched) rough path terms are already having been defined.

The lemma mentioned in the second step is proved thanks to the Lyons-Victoir extension theorem
[
34
]
; in particular, this means that the bracket extension generated by this procedure is highly non-unique and non-canonical. The point of view taken in this paper is slightly different: the bracket extension, simple or full, will be fixed and part of the original data, since subsequent constructions will explicitly depend on it. In practical (stochastic) cases it is realistic to hope that the bracket terms can be defined canonically through the same mechanism which is used to define the original branched rough path. The main purpose of the bracket extension is the following result:

Theorem 1.13

(Change of variable formula for RDE solutions
[
30
, Theorem 5.3.11]
)
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
be a bracket extension of
\boldsymbol{X}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
, and
\boldsymbol{Y}
be a solution to (
28
) (driven by the original rough path
\boldsymbol{X}
). For
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{e})
we have (at the trace level)

\begin{split}g(Y)_{st}=&\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{k}g(Y)F^{k}_{a}(Y)\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{X}^{a}\\
&+\sum_{n=2}^{\lfloor p\rfloor}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}g(Y)F^{k_{1}}_{{t}_{1}}\cdots F^{k_{n}}_{{t}_{n}}(Y)\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({t}_{1}\cdots{t}_{n})}\end{split}

(41)

where the
{t}_{i}
’s range in
\mathcal{T}^{A}
, the
k_{i}
’s in
[e]
and the controlled integrands are defined using (
30
). In particular, for a simple bracket extension
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
of
\boldsymbol{X}
and
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{d})

g(X)_{st}=\sum_{n=1}^{\lfloor p\rfloor}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{a_{1}\ldots a_{n}}g(X)\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})}

(42)

where the
a_{i}
’s range in
A
.

Note how in the above change of variable formulae the only terms of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
(and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
) that are needed to define the rough integrals are those
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\widehat{}{f}}
with
\widehat{}{f}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{A}
in which the only new possible vertex labelled with an element of
\widehat{A}\setminus A
is the root: this is because the trace of
\widehat{X}{}
is only defined in terms of forests with such a labelling, and because integrands are
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled (as opposed to, more generally,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
-controlled). However, it still makes more sense to consider the whole bracket extension - which enables us to consider
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{{f}}
for any
{f}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{A}
— since the terms in which the new labels appear in higher vertices of
{f}
will become relevant in the next section when defining the lift of a controlled path.

Remark 1.14
.

Note how, in light of (
27
), (
41
) and (
42
) respectively define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
- and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
-controlled paths that are distinct to the
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled ones given by the formulae (
23
) and (
24
). Although the latter do not require the bracket extension, the former have the advantage of vanishing on proper forests, precisely the property that is required to represent them as integrals. In the second case
g(X)
can be
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
-controlled as

and zero on other types of trees. Here

is the order of the automorphism group of the corresponding forest with

\mathcal{N}(b_{1}\cdots b_{j})^{-1}
will be replaced with
j!^{-1}
. This is a special case of
Remark 1.8
.

2
The extended lift of a controlled path

In this section we will show how, given
\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
endowed with bracket extension
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
, one can lift
\boldsymbol{H}\in D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e})
to a rough path
\uparrow_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}\in C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{e})
in a canonical fashion. Since this lift depends not only on
\boldsymbol{X}
but also on
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
, we will assume the bracket extension to be fixed and part of the initial data; we will denote
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
the simple bracket extension determined by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
, which will be sufficient for certain constructions. Special attention will be given to the case of pushforwards, i.e. in which
\boldsymbol{H}
is given by a smooth function of the trace
X
, for which a canonical simple bracket extension can be defined which only depends on
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
.

We must begin by imposing a new condition on our bracket extension: the relations that define it need to be required not only when the new label is on the root, but also higher up. For instance, one might expect it to be the case that

This type of requirement does not appear in
[
30
]
, since it is not necessary to define integrals against the bracket, which only involve bracket labels at the root; it will however be needed when pushing the bracket forward in
Theorem 2.10
.
Given labelled forests
{f},{g}
we denote
{f}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}_{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt{g}
the forest obtained by grafting each root of
{f}
onto the vertex
\nu
of
{g}
; when we write
\nu\in{g}
we allow for the additional case
\nu=-
which means we are simply multiplying
{f}{g}
. Note that by taking
{g}=\varnothing,\ \nu=-
below we recover the definition of ordinary bracket (
40
).

Bracket consistency.

\displaystyle\langle{f}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

for

, and

.

(45)

where the last expression is defined by extending the
\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}_{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt
operator linearly. We will refer to bracket extensions that satisfy this property as
consistent
, and denote respectively

\widehat{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A}),\qquad\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})

(46)

the set of consistent bracket extensions and the set of consistent simple bracket extensions (i.e. in which the defining relation is only required with
{f}
a product of single vertices) of their restriction to elements of
C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
. These sets are respectively contained in
C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\smash{\mathbb R^{\widetilde{A}})}
and
C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\smash{\mathbb R^{\widehat{A}})}
. It is not difficult to construct bracket extensions that violate consistency: indeed, given any consistent bracket extension of a (homogeneously graded)
[3,4)\ni p
-rough path, it is possible to generate an inconsistent one simply by adding non-trivial paths of bounded
p/3
-variation to the terms indexed with trees
\scriptscriptstyle{c}
\scriptscriptstyle{(ab)}
. The condition must therefore be required. We will henceforth assume
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
(
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
) to be a consistent (simple) bracket extension of
\boldsymbol{X}
; while this will not be needed in
Theorem 2.8
, it will in the other main theorem of this section,
Theorem 2.10
, and in general is a desirable property.

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{s<u,v<w<t}\mathrm{d}H^{i}_{u}\mathrm{d}H^{j}_{v}\mathrm{d}H^{k}_{w}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f},{g},{h}\in\mathcal{F}^{A}\\
|{f}|+|{g}|+|{h}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}^{i}_{{f};s}\boldsymbol{H}^{j}_{{g};s}\boldsymbol{H}^{k}_{{h};s}}{\mathcal{N}({f})\mathcal{N}({g})\mathcal{N}({h})}\int_{s<u,v<w<t}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{su}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{g}_{sv}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{h}_{sw}.

We now need to simplify the terms such as
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{u}
: if
{f}=[k]_{a}
is a tree, (
17
) suggests substituting
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{su}=\boldsymbol{X}^{k}_{su}\mathrm{d}X^{a}_{u}
. If
{f}
is not a tree we can use the bracket extension to express
\boldsymbol{X}^{f}
as a sum of terms of the form
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{t}
with
{t}\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{A}
, using (
40
). We can therefore perform the substitution

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{f}_{su}=\mathrm{d}\sum_{({f})_{\mathrm{CK}}}\langle[{f}_{(1)}]_{({f}_{(2)})},\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{su}\rangle=\sum_{({f})_{\mathrm{CK}}}\boldsymbol{X}^{{f}_{(1)}}_{su}\mathrm{d}\widehat{X}^{({f}_{(2)})}_{u}

where, as usual,
\boldsymbol{X}^{({t})}=0
if
{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}
is not a single vertex. Doing the same for
{g},{h}
we are can conclude the calculation above as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}_{st}\hskip-11.19443pt\hbox{\hskip 5.89041pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.89041pt\raisebox{2.58334pt}{\hbox{\scriptsize\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to16.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.78145pt\lower-3.54839pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

cannot avoided: this is because the vertex is not a leaf.

Remark 2.2

(Ordered shuffle)
.

It is instructive to see how this construction specifies to the ordered shuffle (originally introduced in
[
32
]
, see also
[
8
]
) when
\boldsymbol{X}
is geometric rough path: in this case we only need to sum over ladder trees, and the above formula becomes

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-40.18843pt}{42.9066pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\rangle

and the ordered shuffle emerges by applying integration by parts, i.e. summing over all possible ways of collapsing the last tree onto linear trees in ways that maintain the ordering (this is the map
\phi_{g}
defined in
[
26
, (4.9)]
, named
\phi
in (
70
) below): in this case this means respecting the ordering of each diagonal segment and the vertical segment of red vertices, which corresponds to the ordered indices in the ordered shuffle.

Remark 2.3
.

The procedure sketched above (and more precisely
Definition 2.4
below) continues to work without modification when
\boldsymbol{H}
is an
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
-controlled path s.t.
\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}}=0
for
{f}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{A}\setminus(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{A}\cup\mathcal{F}^{A})
. This means, for example, that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
-driven RDE solutions can be lifted (note that
\varnothing\in\mathcal{F}^{A}
, so we are allowing
\boldsymbol{H}_{\varnothing}\neq 0
). Another example of this idea will be used when constructing bracket extensions of the lift.

However, when
\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}}\neq 0
for some proper forest with at least one label in
\widehat{A}\setminus A
, or even
\widetilde{A}\setminus A
, we do not see a way in which the lift can be performed with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
alone. An example would be when
\boldsymbol{H}
is given by a smooth function of
\widehat{X}
, or even of
\widetilde{X}
. What would be needed in this case is a bracket extension of a bracket extension of
\boldsymbol{X}
, and there is no reason why the data of such a rough path, whose terms are indexed by “forests labelled with forests labelled with
A
-labelled forests” should be contained in
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
. For instance, the following identity

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\big),\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle

(49)

which is easy to show directly using (
44
), shows how “bracketing” cannot be considered an associative operation (though it is for quasi-geometric rough paths, discussed in
section 3
below). Similar examples with more indices suggest that even
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
is not sufficient to express evaluations of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
against forests in terms of ones against trees.

In order to obtain a rough path that is rich enough to define lifts of all of its controlled paths, one would have to iterate the bracket extension only a finite number of times, after which further bracket extensions would be negligible (this is because the minimum regularity of the new trace terms in each iteration always increases by one). This, however, is not needed in the applications we have in mind.

We are ready to define the lift construction precisely. This will take some work, and it is convenient to establish some notation. Denote the labelling of a forest
{g}
by
\nu\mapsto\ell_{g}(\nu)
. Given a forest
{g}
, forests
{h}^{\nu}
and labels
a^{\nu}
for each vertex
\nu\in{g}
we denote
\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\{{g};(a^{\nu},{h}^{\nu})^{\nu}\}
the forest constructed by performing the following for each vertex
\nu\in{g}
: (re)label it
a^{\nu}
and then graft the forest
{h}^{\nu}
onto it (i.e. connect the root of each tree in
{h}^{\nu}
to
\nu
by adding an edge). For instance, the term appearing in the earlier example can be written as (assume to have fixed a cut for each
{f},{g},{h}
)
