\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\Big\}

where
k^{\nu}={f},{g},{h}
according to the numbering of nodes
\nu
that starts at the top left node. To make calculations more readable, we will omit the upper bound on collections of forests, with the understanding that our sums are finite since they only contain terms
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{f}_{st}
with
|{f}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}
. The following result should be compared with the recursive formula
[
24
, Remark 8.7]
, which however does not use the bracket extension, and cannot therefore apply to the case in which the controlled path being lifted does not vanish on proper forests. Recall that an
almost (branched) rough path

\boldsymbol{Z}
is a map satisfying the conditions of
Definition 1.5
except for the fact in the multiplicativity requirement we only require approximate equality,
\boldsymbol{Z}_{st}\approx\boldsymbol{Z}_{su}\star\boldsymbol{Z}_{ut}
(note that this also means we can drop the truncation at
{\lfloor p\rfloor}
). A fundamental fact about almost rough paths
\boldsymbol{Z}
is that there exists a unique branched rough path
\boldsymbol{X}
that is
close
to
\boldsymbol{Z}
, i.e. s.t.
\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\approx\boldsymbol{Z}_{st}
, see
[
26
, Proposition 2.16]
, slightly reformulated from
[
24
, Theorem 7.7]
, itself an adaptation of the original
[
35
, Theorem 3.3.1]
to the ramified setting.

Definition 2.4

(Branched lift of a controlled path)
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\widehat{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
restricting to
\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
and
\boldsymbol{H}\in D_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\mathbb R^{e})
we define
\big(\!\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}\big)_{st}^{\bullet^{k}}=H^{k}
and for
{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{e}
with
\#{t}\geq 2

\big(\!\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}\big)^{t}_{st}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{{f}^{\nu}\}^{\nu\in{t}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{A}\setminus\{\varnothing\}\\
({f}^{\nu})_{\mathrm{CK}}\end{subarray}}\Big(\prod_{\nu\in{t}}\mathcal{N}({f}^{\nu})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}^{\nu};s}^{\ell_{t}(\nu)}\Big)\big\langle\!\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\big\{{t};(({f}^{\nu}_{(2)}),{f}^{\nu}_{(1)})^{\nu\in{t}}\big\},\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\big\rangle

(50)

and extending to
\mathcal{F}^{e}
with products. We define
\uparrow_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}
to be the unique rough path close to
\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}
, i.e. s.t.
{(\uparrow_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H})_{st}\approx(\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H})_{st}}
(this requires
Theorem 2.8
below).

The sum in (
50
) is taken over all
A
-labelled forests
{f}^{\nu}
such that
\sum_{\nu\in{t}}|{f}^{\nu}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}
, with
\nu
a vertex of
{t}
, and additionally using Sweedler notation for each
{f}^{\nu}
, i.e. summing over all admissible cuts of each. Also note that
({f}_{(2)}^{\nu})
is a label in
\widehat{A}
, while
{f}_{(1)}^{\nu}
is an
A
-labelled forest, and we are only summing over terms for which
{f}_{(2)}^{\nu}
is a single vertex or a non-trivial proper forest. Before proving the main result of this section we focus on a couple of special cases in which the full bracket extension is actually not needed; for an example in which it generally is, one can take the controlled path to be given by a smooth function of an RDE solution.

Example 2.5

(Lifts of rough integrals and RDEs)
.

When the controlled path is given by a rough integral against
\boldsymbol{X}
, and in particular an
\boldsymbol{X}
-driven RDE solution, its rough path lift only requires
\boldsymbol{X}
and not its bracket extension; this is because the controlled path vanishes on proper forests. Therefore, the only
{f}^{\nu}
’s considered in the sum of (
50
) are trees, and the only cuts considered in the
({f}^{\nu})_{\mathrm{CK}}
’s are those that disconnect the root from the rest of the tree
{f}^{\nu}
. The
\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
terms are then just given by growing
[e]
-labelled trees out of all vertices of
{t}
:

\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\bigg)^{t}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{{f}^{\nu}\}^{\nu\in{t}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{A}\setminus\{\varnothing\}\\
a^{\nu}\in A\end{subarray}}\Big(\prod_{\nu\in{t}}\mathcal{N}({f}^{\nu})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}^{\nu},a^{\nu};s}^{\ell_{t}(\nu)}\Big)\big\langle\!\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\big\{{t};(a^{\nu},{f}^{\nu})^{\nu\in{t}}\big\},\boldsymbol{X}_{st}\big\rangle.

(51)

The expression for the
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled path of an RDE
\boldsymbol{Y}
in terms of its trace (
30
) can be substituted in this formula. RDE lifts have been studied in a more quantitative manner for geometric rough paths
[
21
, §10.4]
under the name
full RDE solutions
, by defining them via smooth approximation of the trace. While such technique permits one to sidestep the algebra in the geometric (finite-dimensional) case, this is not possible for non-geometric branched rough paths, whose terms cannot be realised as limits of Stieltjes integrals of regularisations of the underlying path.

Note how this means that when we write an RDE (
28
), we are viewing the solution
\boldsymbol{Y}
not just as an
\boldsymbol{X}
-controlled path, but also as a rough path in its own right. The following statement (which is proved for controlled integrands in
[
8
, Theorem 2.21]
,
[
1
, Theorem 1.17]
) can be verified using the Davie characterisation (
31
).

Proposition 2.6

(Associativity for RDEs)
.

The system of two RDEs (the second of which is driven by the solution of the first)

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Y}=V(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}\qquad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Z}=W(Z)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Y}

is equivalent to the single
\boldsymbol{X}
-driven RDE

\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Y}\\
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Z}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}V(Y)\\
W(Z)V(Y)\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}.

Example 2.7

(Pushforwards)
.

When the controlled path is
f(X)
(
24
) we call its rough path lift the
pushforward

f_{*}\boldsymbol{X}
. It only depends on the simple bracket extension
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
: this is because the only forests
{f}
over which we need to sum in (
50
) are products of single vertices, and as a consequence all the trees
\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}
are already indexed by letters in
\widetilde{A}
. Using the alternative controlled path of (
1.14
) results in the same definition of
f_{*}\boldsymbol{X}
: this identity actually holds at the level of almost rough paths, which in both cases is given by

\begin{split}\upharpoonleft_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\!f(X)^{{t}}_{st}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\nu}\\

where we are summing over tuples

and

\scriptstyle{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu}}

where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu}=(\alpha^{\nu}_{1}\ldots\alpha^{\nu}_{n})
. The presence of the factorials is due to the fact that we are summing over tuples, not forests, and that over
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\nu}
individually: this change of variable and factor uses an argument involving binomial coefficients explained in the proof of
Theorem 2.10
.

Theorem 2.8
.

\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}
is almost multiplicative, and
\uparrow_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}
therefore defines a branched rough path.

This does not require bracket consistency. We write out the string of identities that prove the first claim, and then carefully comment on each one (this includes explaining the notation used). When
{t}
is a single vertex, the statement follows from that of
H
being a path. For
{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{A}
with
\#{t}\geq 2
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{({t})_{\mathrm{CK}}}\big(\!\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}\big)^{{t}_{(1)}}_{su}\big(\!\upharpoonleft_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\boldsymbol{H}\big)^{{t}_{(2)}}_{ut}

\displaystyle\approx{}

\displaystyle\sum_{({t})_{\mathrm{CK}},({g}^{\mu})_{\mathrm{CK}},({h}^{\nu})_{\mathrm{CK}}}\Big(\prod_{\mu\in{t}_{(1)}}\mathcal{N}({g}^{\mu})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{g}^{\mu};s}^{\ell_{t}(\mu)}\Big)\big\langle\!\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\big\{{t}_{(1)};(({g}^{\mu}_{(2)}),{g}^{\mu}_{(1)})^{\mu}\big\},\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{su}\big\rangle

(53)

\displaystyle\hphantom{= \sum_{ ({t})_\mathrm{CK}, ({g}^\nu)_\mathrm{CK}, ({h}^\nu)_\mathrm{CK}}}\Big(\prod_{\nu\in{t}_{(2)}}\mathcal{N}({h}^{\nu})^{-1}\boldsymbol{H}_{{h}^{\nu};u}^{\ell_{t}(\nu)}\Big)\big\langle\!\mathop{\scalebox{2.2}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$\ast$}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\big\{{t}_{(2)};(({h}^{\nu}_{(2)}),{h}^{\nu}_{(1)})^{\nu}\big\},\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{ut}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\#f^{-1}(k,j,{h})={}

\displaystyle\text{\textdelta}(\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}k,j\otimes{h})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(j)^{-1}\mathcal{N}(h)^{-1}\langle\Delta_{\mathrm{CK}}k,j\otimes{h}\rangle

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(j)^{-1}\mathcal{N}(h)^{-1}\langle k,j\star{h}\rangle

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}(k)}{\mathcal{N}(j)\mathcal{N}(h)}\text{\textdelta}(k,j\star{h})

and

is the number of times that
(k,j,{h})\in A

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{\alpha\gamma}f^{i}\partial_{\beta}f^{j}+\partial_{\alpha}f^{i}\partial_{\beta\gamma}f^{j})(X_{s})\widetilde{X}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}_{st}

\displaystyle\approx{}

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{\alpha}f^{i}\partial_{\beta}f^{j}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}(\partial_{\alpha\gamma}f^{i}\partial_{\beta}f^{j}+\partial_{\alpha}f^{i}\partial_{\beta\gamma}f^{j})(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{X}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}.

where we have crucially used bracket consistency (
47
)

(and similar, with labels permuted). This and a simpler calculation for the order-3 bracket terms imply

\displaystyle f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(ij)}_{st}

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{\alpha}f^{i}\partial_{\beta}f^{j}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}(\partial_{\alpha\gamma}f^{i}\partial_{\beta}f^{j}+\partial_{\alpha}f^{i}\partial_{\beta\gamma}f^{j})(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{X}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)},

\displaystyle f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(ijk)}_{st}

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{\alpha}f^{i}\partial_{\beta}f^{j}\partial_{\gamma}f^{k}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}.

where the exact equalities come from the respective approximate equalities and the fact that the left and right hand sides are both increments of paths, so that the uniqueness claim of
[
35
, Theorem 3.3.1]
implies they must coincide.

In the following theorem we will adopt the notation used for shuffles introduced in
[
8
]
; to summarise here,
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
means that we are considering all possible ways of unshuffling the tuple
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
into tuples
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{n}
, with each tuple possibly empty (unless otherwise indicated). Note how bracket consistency is crucial for the following result.

Theorem 2.10

(Simple bracket extension of pushforwards)
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{d},\mathbb R^{e})
, then we can define a simple bracket extension of
f_{*}\boldsymbol{X}
by

f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(k_{1}\ldots k_{m})}=\sum_{|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}|>0}\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}|!\cdots|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}|!}\boldsymbol{\int}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{1}}f^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{m}}f^{k_{m}}(X)\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{1}\ldots\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{m})}

(60)

where we are summing over non-empty tuples (with
|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}|+\cdots+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}|\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}
),
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{1}\ldots\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{m}
denotes their concatenation, and the lift of the integral is performed according to
Example 2.5
.

That
f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
agrees with
f_{*}\boldsymbol{X}
on
\mathcal{F}^{d}
has already been checked in
Example 2.7
. Also note that, even though the integral in (
60
) is
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
-controlled, it can be lifted using
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
alone, since it is an integral — no bracket terms in excess of those already contained in
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
are needed. What remains to be shown is that

\langle\ll\hskip-1.99997ptk_{1}\ldots k_{m}\hskip-1.99997pt\gg\hskip-1.99997pt\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}_{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt{t},f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle=\langle\hbox to40.41pt{\vbox to16.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.36 0 C 2.36 1.3 1.3 2.36 0 2.36 C -1.3 2.36 -2.36 1.3 -2.36 0 C -2.36 -1.3 -1.3 -2.36 0 -2.36 C 1.3 -2.36 2.36 -1.3 2.36 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.033pt}{4.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-3.00003pt\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.36 L 15.75 -2.36}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}_{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt{t},f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle.

Since the same proof works independently of the position of the vertex
\nu
, we will prove it only at the ground level, i.e.

\langle\ll\hskip-1.99997ptk_{1}\ldots k_{m}\hskip-1.99997pt\gg,f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle=f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(k_{1}\ldots k_{m})}.

is the definition in the statement.

Here we have expanded the integral: on the first line we have written the zero-th order term in its expansion, and the sum in the second line, in which
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is the concatenation
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{1}\ldots{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{m}
, is the sum of it’s Gubinelli derivatives. Note that the
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{l}
’s are allowed to be empty, as long as their concatenation is not. The missing step is

is equivalent to a sum on

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{h}
are all empty, and since

was always positive, the condition in the new sum is satisfied.

This concludes the proof.
∎

In light of the above, we can push forward not only rough paths, but also simple bracket extensions:

Definition 2.11

(Pushforward of a simple bracket extension)
.

We will call
f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
the
pushforward
of the simple bracket extension
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
.

3
A characterisation of quasi-geometric rough paths

While most of the literature on rough paths distinguishes between geometric and branched rough paths, there is an intermediate type that is general enough to include Itô integration, but defined on a Hopf algebra that is simpler to describe than the Connes-Kreimer one: the
quasi-shuffle algebra
, original to
[
27
]
. Rough paths defined on the quasi shuffle algebra are called
quasi-geometric
. Although the topic has been known about for some time
[
25
]
, it has only recently appeared in the literature
[
5
]
.

In this section we show how quasi-geometric rough paths can be characterised as consistent bracket extensions of branched rough paths whose non-simple terms vanish. Geometric rough paths admit a similar characterisation (alternative to the one provided in
[
26
, §4.1]
), namely, they coincide with those branched rough paths that admit a consistent bracket extension that is trivial. The main reason for which we are interested in quasi-geometric rough paths is that the change of variable formula for RDE solutions simplifies into one that is analogous to the change of variable formula for functions, which could make it possible to adapt the transfer principle of
section 5
to define RDEs on manifolds.

We begin with a very brief review of the quasi-shuffle algebra of
[
27
]
. While these are usually defined w.r.t. a bracket, we will treat the most general case of the free bracket; any rough path that is defined on the quasi-shuffle algebra w.r.t. a particular bracket can be defined on the free quasi-shuffle algebra, with the only drawback that we may have some redundant coordinates (e.g. if
X
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion and we want to define a rough path by Itô integration we have to set
X^{(\alpha\beta)}=[X]^{\alpha\beta}=0
when
\alpha\neq\beta
instead of just setting
(\alpha\beta)=0
). The advantage is that we can speak of
the
quasi-shuffle algebra, without having to specify a bracket. In this section the core components of our rough path will have trace valued in
\mathbb R^{d}
. All of this means that our quasi-shuffle algebra
T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}
has as its underlying set the tensor algebra not over
\mathbb R^{d}
, but over
\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d}\coloneqq\mathbb R^{\widetilde{[d]}}
(notation for
\widetilde{[d]}
as in (
2
),(
34
)). Note that
\widetilde{[d]}
is a countably infinite set despite
[d]
being a finite one; this will not be an issue once we are dealing with rough paths, since they will be defined on the algebra truncated at some order, considering that the weighting on
\widetilde{[d]}
is given by cardinality of multisets (counting repetitions). We will use round brackets to denote multisets, unless the multiset only has one element, in which case brackets will be omitted. Generators (i.e. elementary tensors) of
T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}
are words of multisets, e.g. for the following words of weight
9

\alpha(\alpha\beta)\gamma\delta\varepsilon(\zeta\zeta\eta)=\alpha(\beta\alpha)\gamma\delta\varepsilon(\zeta\eta\zeta)\neq\alpha(\beta\alpha)\gamma\varepsilon\delta(\zeta\eta\zeta),\qquad\alpha,\ldots,\eta\in[d].

The quasi-shuffle product is defined recursively by declaring the empty word to be the identity element for it, and for
w,z
words in the alphabet
\widetilde{[d]}
and
a,b\in\widetilde{[d]}

wa\,\widetilde{\shuffle}\,zb=\{wa\,\widetilde{\shuffle}\,z\}b+\{w\,\widetilde{\shuffle}\,zb\}a+\{w\,\widetilde{\shuffle}\,z\}(ab)

(68)

where braces are used to specify the order of operations (quasi-shuffle and concatenation). Here
(ab)\coloneqq a\cup b
as multisets, e.g. if
a=(\alpha\alpha\beta)
,
b=(\beta\beta\gamma)
we have
(ab)=(\alpha\alpha\beta\beta\beta\gamma)
; the same notation will be used for
n
-fold unions. The shuffle product admits the following non-recursive expression
[
15
, p.9]
:

a_{1}\ldots a_{m}\,\widetilde{\shuffle}\,a_{m+1}\ldots a_{m+n}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\vee n\leq k\leq m+n\\
f\colon[m+n]\twoheadrightarrow[k]\\
f|_{[1,m]},f|_{[m+1,n]}\nearrow\end{subarray}}\Big(\bigcup_{i\in f^{-1}(1)}a_{i}\Big)\ldots\Big(\bigcup_{i\in f^{-1}(k)}a_{i}\Big),\quad a_{i}\in\widetilde{[d]}

(69)

where we are summing over all surjections from the set with
m+n
elements to the set of
k
elements,
k
ranging from
m\vee n
to
m+n
, and s.t.
f(1)<\ldots<f(m)
and
f(m+1)<\ldots<f(m+n)
. An example is

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})\,\widetilde{\shuffle}\,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}=

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})\beta_{2}+\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})\beta_{2}+\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})+\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle+\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\beta_{2})+\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\beta_{2})+\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\beta_{1})\beta_{2}+\beta_{1}(\beta_{2}\alpha_{1})(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+(\alpha_{1}\beta_{1})(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})\beta_{2}+(\alpha_{1}\beta_{1})\beta_{2}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle+(\alpha_{1}\beta_{1})(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\beta_{2})

where we have used indentation to separate the sum by cardinality of the codomain’s surjection: 4 (shuffles), 3 and 2. The coproduct on
T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}
is identical to the one for the shuffle algebra, i.e. deconcatenation
\Delta_{\otimes}
, and so are the unit and counit. These operations turns
T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}
into a Hopf algebra, whose antipode is described explicitly in
[
27
, Theorem 3.2]
.

For a self-contained treatment of quasi-geometric rough paths we refer to
[
5
]
. Quasi-geometric rough paths are defined analogously to branched and geometric ones, with the role of the Connes-Kreimer or shuffle bialgebra played by the quasi-shuffle bialgebra. Moreover, it has been shown that rough paths can be defined in a uniform manner on a large class of Hopf algebras, with the core theorems remaining true
[
9
, §4.2]
; quasi-geometric rough paths are just another instance of this principle. Also, it will be convenient to keep thinking of quasi-geometric rough paths as branched rough paths, so that we do not have to switch settings, but may simply make use of the simplifications that the quasi-shuffle algebra makes possible. How this is done will become clear after we give brief survey of the maps between shuffle, quasi-shuffle and nonplanar forest Hopf algebras that appear in the following commutative diagram of graded Hopf algebra morphisms:

\hbox to282.72pt{\vbox to58.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 141.35588pt\lower-29.83086pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-141.35588pt}{-21.52533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{{}}}{{{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(70)

•

The epimorphism
\phi
on the left is used in
[
26
, §4.1]
to show how geometric rough paths canonically define branched rough paths: namely, given a geometric rough path
\boldsymbol{Z}
we can define a branched one
\boldsymbol{X}\coloneqq\phi^{*}\boldsymbol{Z}
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{X}^{f}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{Z}^{\phi({f})}
; this amounts to expressing the branched components using integration by parts. In the following we will be using the convenient notation
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\phi}\coloneqq\phi^{*}\boldsymbol{Z}
and similar.
\phi
is characterised as the unique algebra morphism s.t.

\phi([{f}]_{\gamma})=\phi({f})\gamma,\qquad{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}

(71)

where
\phi({f})\gamma
denotes the word obtained by juxtaposing the word
\phi({f})
and
\gamma
. The other map labelled
\phi
is defined in the same way on the enlarged alphabet. Intuitively, these maps sum over all ways of collapsing a forest onto the vertical axis in ways that preserve the ordering, and then reading off the labels from top to bottom to obtain a word.

•

The map
\widetilde{\phi}
is the unique algebra morphism satisfying the same condition as (
71
), but is distinct from
\phi
in that
\widetilde{\phi}({f}{g})=\phi({f})\widetilde{\shuffle}\phi({g})
with the quasi-shuffle product.

•

There is also an inclusion (not drawn)
\iota\colon T(\mathbb R^{d})_{\shuffle}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{d}_{\mathrm{CK}}
(and similar ones
T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\shuffle}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{CK}}^{d}
,
T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{CK}}^{d}
, which we will also call
\iota
), which maps the word
\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}
to the ladder tree with vertices labelled
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
from top to bottom;
\iota
is a right inverse to
\phi
and a coalgebra morphism but not an algebra one.
\iota
is used to check weather a branched rough path comes from a geometric one, which occurs if and only if
\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{X}^{\iota\circ\phi}
.

•

The Hopf algebra monomorphism
\psi
is used in
[
26
, §4.2]
for the following purpose: given an
\mathbb R^{d}
-valued branched rough path
\boldsymbol{X}
, it is of interest to define a geometric rough path
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}
on the larger space
\mathbb R^{\mathcal{T}^{d}}
(this means that its trace is indexed by
[d]
-labelled trees) with the property that
\boldsymbol{X}
-driven RDEs can be equivalently expressed as
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}
-driven ones.
\psi
is used to formulate the condition, namely
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\psi({f})}=\boldsymbol{X}^{f}
, that
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}
must satisfy for
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}
to contain the data encoded in
\boldsymbol{X}
. It is characterised as the unique algebra morphism s.t.

\psi(t)=t+\sum_{(\widetilde{t})_{\mathrm{CK}}}\psi(t_{(1)})\otimes t_{(2)},\qquad t\in\mathcal{T}^{d}

(72)

where we emphasise that the coproduct is reduced. This map cannot be used to actually define
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}
, a task first achieved through a recursive procedure (similar to the one used for defining the bracket
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}
) with calls to the Lyons-Victoir extension theorem, which uses the axiom of choice. A method which, unlike the previous, is constructive (but still not canonical) was defined in
[
43
]
. Another, more algebraic, method to obtain a constructive Itô-Stratonovich formula was identified in
[
2
]
; here the authors use the surprising fact, proved independently in
[
18
,
10
]
, that the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra is free, i.e. isomorphic to the tensor algebra, over some set of trees (which however also appears not to be canonical). A detailed comparison of the various Itô-Stratonovich formulae in branched rough path theory is performed in
[
7
]
.

•

[
27
]
showed the existence of a canonical isomorphism,
Hoffman’s exponential

\exp\colon T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\shuffle}\to T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}
between the quasi-shuffle and shuffle Hopf algebras over the same alphabet. Its significance for rough paths is that it makes it possible to canonically obtain a geometric rough path
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}\coloneqq\exp^{*}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}})
from a quasi-geometric one
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
with the property that equations
\mathrm{d}Y=F(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
are equivalent to ones
\mathrm{d}Y=F_{\log^{*}(({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}))}(Y)\mathrm{d}\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}
, where
\log\coloneqq\exp^{-1}
and we are summing over multisets
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\in\widetilde{[d]}
(where
F_{\boldsymbol{a}}(Y)
for
\boldsymbol{a}
a word in the alphabet
\widetilde{[d]}
denotes the corresponding term in the Davie expansion — since
\log^{*}
maps letters to linear combinations of words, e.g.
\log^{*}((\alpha\beta))=(\alpha\beta)-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\beta
, this generates correction terms involving the derivatives of
F
). This can be shown by proving that the Davie expansions for the two equations coincide, and generalises the Itô-Stratonovich correction formula for SDEs.

•

The
arborified exponential

\mathrm{Aexp}
, described in
[
3
, Theorem 2]
, is a Hopf algebra automorphism that makes the square commute.

We now transition to the main focus of this section: describing those branched rough paths that are
(quasi-)geometric
, in terms of their brackets. To preliminarily identify those branched rough paths that come from a quasi-geometric one we can proceed as done for geometric ones, but using the map
\widetilde{\phi}
of (
70
): namely, a branched rough path
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})
is
quasi-geometric
if
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{\vphantom{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\iota\circ\widetilde{\phi}}
. This is equivalent to the definition of
[
5
]
, i.e. we can define it as a functional on the quasi-shuffle Hopf algebra as
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}\coloneqq\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\iota}
, and it holds that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\iota\circ\widetilde{\phi}}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}{}^{\widetilde{\phi}}
(
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
is determined by
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}
by a quasi-integration by parts rule). Indeed,
\boldsymbol{Z}
is group-valued since, for words
w,z

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}{}^{w}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}{}^{z}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\iota(w)}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\iota(z)}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{\iota(w)\iota(z)}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}{}^{\phi(\iota(w)\iota(z))}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}{}^{\phi\circ\iota(w)\widetilde{\shuffle}\phi\circ\iota(z)}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}{}^{w\widetilde{\shuffle}z}

as
\phi
is a left inverse to
\iota
.
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}
is multiplicative because the Connes-Kreimer coproduct on ladder trees corresponds to deconcatenation of the corresponding word. The next result states that a branched rough path defined on
\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d}
is quasi-geometric if and only if it is closed under taking the simple bracket extension, and admits trivial non-simple bracket extension. The statement will make these assertions precise. It may be viewed as an extension of
[
5
, Theorem 4.16]
, in which it was already shown that a quasi-geometric rough path defines a simple bracket extension. We will preliminarily need to consider forests indexed by multisets of the set
\widetilde{[d]}
, i.e.
\widetilde{\widetilde{[d]}}
; an example of such a label is
(\alpha(\beta\gamma))\varepsilon
. The set of forests indexed by such labels may be denoted
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\widetilde{[d]}}
. Also, recall the notation (
46
).

Theorem 3.1

(Characterisation of (quasi-)geometric rough paths)
.

The following are equivalent:

1.

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})
is quasi-geometric;

2.

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})
defines an element of
\widehat{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})
in the following way:
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{f}=0
for all
{f}
that have at least one label in
\widehat{\widetilde{[d]}}\setminus\widetilde{\widetilde{[d]}}
, and the simple bracket extension is given by joining labels, i.e. a label of the form
(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})
with
a_{k}=(\alpha^{1}_{k}\cdots\alpha^{m_{k}}_{k})
,
\alpha^{i}_{j}\in[d]
, are set to
(\alpha^{1}_{1}\cdots\alpha^{m_{1}}_{1}\cdots\cdots\alpha^{1}_{n}\cdots\alpha^{m_{n}}_{n})
. Performing such substitutions at all vertices of
{f}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\widetilde{[d]}}
yields a forest in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}
, against which
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
can be evaluated.

Similarly, the following are equivalent:

1.

\boldsymbol{X}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{d})
is geometric;

2.

Setting
\widehat{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{f}=0
for
{f}
having at least one label in
\widehat{[d]}\setminus[d]
defines an element of
\widehat{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{d})
.

We will only give a proof of the characterisation of quasi-geometric rough paths; the characterisation of geometric ones follows an analogous, and indeed simpler, procedure. We begin with 1
\Rightarrow
2. We must show the bracket relations, which in this case read

\langle{f}\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.90709pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

as either the total cut or the cut disconnecting the root from the rest of the tree, with at least one cut of the latter type overall. Moreover, the label

is defined by the label-joining rule expressed in the statement. By quasi-geometricity, these relations can be written as

which uses an

is, and from the general fact that extending a rough path to a new alphabet trivially also preserves the rough path properties.

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}_{\nu}\hskip-1.00006pt{g},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\rangle

where we have used the consistent bracket relations (
73
), the inductive hypothesis, and in the last step (
74
).
∎

Example 3.2

(Quasi-geometricity for
3\leq p<4
)
.

When
3\leq p<4
the only obstruction to quasi geometricity of a consistent full bracket extension is its evaluations on the labels
(\!\!\smash{\hbox to19.4pt{\vbox to15.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.20026pt\lower-4.4094pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\!\!)
: these vanish if and only if
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
is quasi-geometric, by (
49
) and the above theorem.

As a consequence we have the next change of variable formula. Note that a self-contained proof of the change of variable formula for functions of
X
can be found in
[
5
]
. We will be considering equations

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{Y}=F_{a}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{a}

(76)

driven by the whole of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
(
a
ranges in
\widetilde{[d]}
). This is a particular case of (
28
) with
A=\widetilde{[d]}
. Here we are summing over multisets
a
, although in the next sections we will be summing over tuples, with symmetrising factors.

Corollary 3.3

(Change of variable formula for quasi-geometric RDEs)
.

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{d})
restricting to
\boldsymbol{X}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{d})
be quasi-geometric, and
Y
be a solution to (
76
). For
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb R^{e})
we have

g(Y)_{st}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{n=1}^{\lfloor p\rfloor}\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}g(Y)F^{k_{1}}_{a_{1}}\cdots F^{k_{n}}_{a_{n}}(Y)\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})}

(77)

where we are summing over
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\widetilde{[d]}
and
(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})
is the label obtained by joining
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
.

This is a straightforward application of [
Theorem 3.1
, 1.
\Rightarrow
2.] to (
41
), where in the latter we are taking
A
to be
\widetilde{[d]}
.
∎

Another consequence is the following formula for the simple bracket extension of an integral against a quasi-geometric rough path. It is essentially the same formula as the one for pushforwards
Theorem 2.10
, but the mechanism behind the proof is quasi-geometricity, as opposed to the special form of the integrands. In particular, the same result cannot be expected to hold for general branched rough paths, even for integrals of one-forms. We note that this is the result on quasi-geometric rough path that will be used in
section 5
; the previous corollary is actually not used.

Theorem 3.4

(Simple bracket extension of quasi-geometric integrals)
.

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})
be quasi-geometric and
\boldsymbol{H}\in D_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}(\mathbb R^{e\times\widetilde{d}})
be
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
-controlled. Then the rough path
\int\!\boldsymbol{H}_{c}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{c}
(defined by
Example 2.5
) admits the following consistent simple bracket extension

\int\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{n}}^{k_{n}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(c_{1}\ldots c_{n})}

(78)

where the lift to an element of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}\in\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],\mathbb R^{A})
is performed in accordance to
Example 2.5
. In particular, the RDE (
76
) can be extended to the RDE

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}{}^{(k_{1}\ldots k_{n})}=F^{k_{1}}_{c_{1}}\cdots F_{c_{n}}^{k_{n}}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(c_{1}\ldots c_{n})}

(79)

which defines a simple bracket extension of
\boldsymbol{Y}
.

First of all, the meaning of (
78
) and of its rough path lift is given by the fact that, by (
23
),
\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{n}}^{k_{n}}
is controlled with Gubinelli derivatives

\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{n}}^{k_{n}}\big)_{f}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{n})\in(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d})^{n}\\
{f}_{1}\cdots{f}_{n}={f}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{N}({f})}{\mathcal{N}({f}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({f}_{n})}\boldsymbol{H}^{k_{1}}_{{f}_{1},c_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}^{k_{n}}_{{f}_{n},c_{n}}.

(80)

As for
Theorem 2.10
, we only argue at the ground level (consistency of the bracket extension follows by the same calculation anywhere else on the tree). We have

\displaystyle\langle\ll\hskip-1.99997ptk_{1}\ldots k_{n}\hskip-1.99997pt\gg,\boldsymbol{\textstyle\int}_{\!s}^{t}\boldsymbol{H}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}\rangle

\displaystyle\approx{}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{n}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}\\
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\widetilde{[d]}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{1},c_{1};s}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{n},c_{n};s}^{k_{n}}}{\mathcal{N}({f}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({f}_{n})}\langle[{f}_{1}]_{c_{1}}\cdots[{f}_{n}]_{c_{n}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\rangle

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{i}},{\boldsymbol{j}})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{i}}|,|{\boldsymbol{j}}|>0\\
{g}_{1},\ldots,{g}_{m},{h}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}\\
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m},b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-m}\in\widetilde{[d]}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}_{{g}_{1},a_{1};s}^{i_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{{g}_{m},a_{m};s}^{i_{m}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{j_{1}}_{b_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}^{j_{n-m}}_{b_{n-m}})_{{h};s}}{\mathcal{N}({g}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({g}_{m})\mathcal{N}({h})}\big\langle\big[[{g}_{1}]_{a_{1}}\cdots[{g}_{m}]_{a_{m}}{h}\big]_{(b_{1}\ldots b_{n-m})},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\big\rangle

(81)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{n}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}\\
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\widetilde{[d]}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{1},c_{1};s}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{n},c_{n};s}^{k_{n}}}{\mathcal{N}({f}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({f}_{n})}\langle[{f}_{1}]_{c_{1}}\cdots[{f}_{n}]_{c_{n}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\rangle

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{i}},{\boldsymbol{j}})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{i}}|,|{\boldsymbol{j}}|>0\\
{g}_{1},\ldots,{g}_{m},{h}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}\\
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m},b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-m}\in\widetilde{[d]}\\
({h}_{1},\ldots,{h}_{n-m})\colon{h}_{1}\cdots{h}_{n-m}={h}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}_{{g}_{1},a_{1};s}^{i_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{{g}_{m},a_{m};s}^{i_{m}}\boldsymbol{H}^{j_{1}}_{{h}_{1},b_{1};s}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}^{j_{n-m}}_{{h}_{n-m},b_{n-m};s}}{\mathcal{N}({g}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({g}_{m})\mathcal{N}({h}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({h}_{n-m})}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023pt\cdot\big\langle\big[[{g}_{1}]_{a_{1}}\cdots[{g}_{m}]_{a_{m}}{h}\big]_{(b_{1}\ldots b_{n-m})},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\big\rangle

(82)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{n}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}\\
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\widetilde{[d]}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{1},c_{1};s}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{n},c_{n};s}^{k_{n}}}{\mathcal{N}({f}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({f}_{n})}\Big\langle[{f}_{1}]_{c_{1}}\cdots[{f}_{n}]_{c_{n}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}((r_{1},\ldots,r_{m}),(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n-m}))\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}(1,\ldots,n)\\
0<m<n\end{subarray}}[[{f}_{r_{1}}]_{c_{r_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{173023}{163840}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{173023}{163840}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{127117}{163840}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{127118}{163840}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}}}}}\cdots[{f}_{r_{m}}]_{c_{r_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}{f}_{q_{1}}\cdots{f}_{q_{n-m}}]_{(c_{q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152540}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152541}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}}}}\ldots c_{q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1920066}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle n-m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1920066}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle n-m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1600957}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle n-m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1800150}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle n-m$}}}}}}})},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\Big\rangle

(83)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f}_{1},\ldots,{f}_{n}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}\\
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\widetilde{[d]}\end{subarray}}\frac{\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{1},c_{1};s}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{{f}_{n},c_{n};s}^{k_{n}}}{\mathcal{N}({f}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{N}({f}_{n})}\langle[{f}_{1}\cdots{f}_{n}]_{(c_{1}\cdots c_{n})},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}_{st}\rangle

(84)

\displaystyle\approx{}

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{H}_{c_{n}}^{k_{n}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(c_{1}\ldots c_{n})}.

(85)

(
81
)

is the definition of bracket polynomial in the simple case (
37
) combined with the rough path lift of an integral (
51
);

(
82
)

involves substituting in the expression for the Gubinelli derivatives of the product (
80
);

(
83
)

re-names the forests
{g}_{k}
and
{h}_{l}
with the common symbols
{f}_{p}
;

(
84
)

follows by replacing the term
[{f}_{1}]_{c_{1}}\cdots[{f}_{n}]_{c_{n}}
with
\iota\circ\widetilde{\phi}([{f}_{1}]_{c_{1}}\cdots[{f}_{n}]_{c_{n}})
, which can be done thanks to the fact that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
is quasi-geometric, and applying (
75
), which results in cancellations that only leave the term
[{f}_{1}\cdots{f}_{n}]_{(c_{1}\cdots c_{n})}
. Finally,

(
85
)

uses again (
80
) and the definition of integral (
26
).∎

We end this section with a couple of examples, which show how the quasi-shuffle algebra, despite not being as large as the Connes-Kreimer algebra, is still capable of expressing a rich variety of stochastic integration theories. An example of a naturally-arising stochastic branched rough path that is not quasi-geometric is given in the author’s PhD thesis
[
16
, Ch. 6]
.

Example 3.5

(
2\leq p<3
)
.

This is the most considered case; the algebra involved is essentially the same as that used in Itô calculus (e.g. see
[
5
, §3.3]
for more details). While Young integration against paths of bounded
2>p
-variation is vacuously geometric, every
[2,3)\ni p
-rough path is quasi-geometric, since the terms
X^{(\alpha\beta)}
can be defined canonically in terms of the
X^{\gamma}
’s as in (
44
), with no extra lifts involved; this is the
bracket
of
[
17
, Definition 5.5]
. Iterated Itô integrals and their relationship with Stratonovich ones fit into this case; these topics were treated prior to the introduction of quasi-shuffles by using Wick products
[
22
,
23
]
. Iterated Itô and Stratonovich integrals can be obtained from one another by using the
\exp
and
\log
, and similarly their branched counterparts by using the arborified versions of
\exp
and
\log
. An example of a stochastic rough path that is not given by Itô integration, but still belongs to this category, is the one defined in
[
41
]
above fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter
H\in(1/3,1/2)
.

Example 3.6

(Itô formulae for the 1-dimensional heat equation,
[
4
, Ch. 4]
)
.

The cited PhD thesis considers the solution of the 1-dimensional stochastic heat equation with additive noise

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{xx}u+\xi\\
u(0,x)=0\end{cases}\quad X_{t}\coloneqq u(t,x),\qquad x\in\mathbb R

although the same techniques would work for many other Gaussian processes, such as fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter
1/4
. The author is then able to reproduce and shed new light on Itô-type formulae for
X
present in the literature (the Burdzy-Swanson formula, the Cheridito-Nualart formula and the quartic variation formula), by defining three distinct
4=p
-quasi-geometric rough paths above
X
, all distinct from the geometric rough path defined canonically by powers of
X
thanks to unidimensionality.

4
Integration against branched rough paths on manifolds

In this section
M
will denote a smooth manifold. We will define
M
-valued rough paths in local coordinates via pushforwards; this was first done in
[
6
]
and subsequently in
[
1
,
8
]
in the context of
3>p
-rough paths and geometric rough paths of low regularity. The only significant difference here is that we must push forward not only the branched rough path, but also a consistent simple bracket extension, which is done using the augmented definition of pushforwards
Definition 2.11
. This is because, first of all, a definition that does not carry the simple bracket extension would not be possible, since it is even necessary to define the non-bracketed pushforward; moreover, it will be needed when defining rough integration. Specifically, this means that, given an atlas
\mathcal{A}
for
M
, we have a collection
\{{{}^{\imath}\!}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\mid\imath\in I\}
with
{{}^{\imath}\!}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\in\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([s_{\imath},t_{\imath}],\mathbb R^{d})
for some
0\leq s_{\imath}<t_{\imath}\leq T
, such that for each index
\imath
there exists a chart
\varphi_{\imath}\in\mathcal{A}
with
{{}^{\imath}\!}X
(the non-bracket portion of the trace) taking values in the range of
\varphi_{\imath}
(so that
\varphi_{\imath}({{}^{\imath}\!}X)
is a path
[s_{\imath},t_{\imath}]\to M
), and such that the compatibility condition

(\varphi_{\jmath}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\imath})_{*}{{}^{\imath}\!}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}={{}^{\jmath}\!}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}

(86)

holds over
[s_{\imath},t_{\imath}]\cap[s_{\jmath},t_{\jmath}]
for all
\imath,\jmath\in I
for which
(s_{\imath},t_{\imath})\cap(s_{\jmath},t_{\jmath})\neq\varnothing
. Proving associativity of pushforwards, i.e.
f_{*}g_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}=(f\circ g)_{*}\boldsymbol{X}
, makes it possible to extend the collection
\{{{}^{\imath}\!}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\}_{\imath}
to the maximal atlas, thus making the definition independent of the original atlas. We will call the set of such objects
\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],M)
.

Before discussing integration, we begin with some background and notation. We now assume that
M
is endowed with a connection (interchangeably referred to as covariant derivative)
\nabla
on its tangent bundle, with Christoffel symbols
\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
; we will not assume
M
to be Levi-Civita, or even torsion-free. The notions that we will need about covariant derivatives are not advanced and, unless otherwise specified, can all be found in
[
33
, Ch.4]
; some care is, however, required, since we will often be considering the
n^{\text{th}}
iterated covariant derivative. We adopt the convention that, if
S,T
are tensor fields and
V
is a vector field

\langle\nabla_{V}T,S\rangle=\langle\nabla T,V\otimes S\rangle,

(87)

i.e. the direction of covariant differentiation occupies the “first slot”; this is the only instance in which we differ from
[
33
, Ch.4]
in terms of notation. Our choice works well with the convention that, in the expression of a tensor, the contravariant part (i.e. a tensor product of the tangent space
TM
) always comes before the covariant part. Furthermore, it has the benefit of not reversing the order of covariant differentiation when iterating
\nabla
, e.g. for another vector field
U
we have

\langle\nabla^{2}T,U\otimes V\otimes S\rangle\coloneqq\langle\nabla\nabla T,U\otimes V\otimes S\rangle=\langle\nabla_{U}\nabla T,V\otimes S\rangle.

Note that this is not equal to
\langle\nabla_{U}\nabla_{V}T,S\rangle
: by the Leibniz rules for
\nabla
w.r.t. to the dual pairing and tensor product

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\nabla T,U\otimes V\otimes S\rangle

\displaystyle=\nabla_{U}\langle\nabla T,V\otimes S\rangle-\langle\nabla T,\nabla_{U}(V\otimes S)\rangle

\displaystyle=\nabla_{U}\langle\nabla T,V\otimes S\rangle-\langle\nabla T,\nabla_{U}V\otimes S\rangle-\langle\nabla T,V\otimes\nabla_{U}S\rangle

while

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{U}\nabla_{V}T,S\rangle

\displaystyle=\nabla_{U}\langle\nabla_{V}T,S\rangle-\langle\nabla_{V}T,\nabla_{U}S\rangle

so that

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{U}\nabla_{V}T,S\rangle

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla\nabla T,U\otimes V\otimes S\rangle=\langle\nabla_{\nabla_{U}V}T,S\rangle.

For a tensor field
T
we define
\nabla^{n}T
inductively by
\nabla\nabla^{n-1}T
; this is obviously associative, i.e.
\nabla\nabla^{n-1}T=\nabla^{n-1}\nabla T
. The most important case is when
T
is a smooth, real-valued function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, for which
\nabla^{n}f\in\Gamma(T^{*}M^{\otimes n})
,
\Gamma
denoting the
C^{\infty}(M)
-module of sections of the vector bundle that comes after it and
T^{*}M
the cotangent bundle of
M
(in this case, taken to its
n
-tensor power). While it is well known that
\nabla^{2}f
is a symmetric tensor if and only if
\nabla
is torsion-free, the same does not hold at higher orders: indeed,
\nabla^{2}f
and
\nabla^{3}f
are symmetric (for general
f
) if and only if
\nabla
is flat and torsion-free
[
31
, Theorem 2.3]
. It is not possible, therefore, to assume symmetry of higher order covariant derivatives of functions, without restricting attention to trivial cases. On the other hand, the symmetrised
n
-order Hessian does not depend on the connection’s torsion, as is evident from (
89
) below.

In keeping with the rest of this article, we will mostly carry out computations in coordinates, which on manifolds are local. Given a chart, we denote
\partial_{\gamma}
the basis elements of
TM
that they define at each point. For a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})
we will denote the operator acting on
C^{\infty}(M)

\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}-\coloneqq\langle\nabla^{n}-,\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\partial_{\gamma_{n}}\rangle.

(88)

\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}f
can be computed recursively by

\nabla_{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}}f=\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\nabla_{\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{n}}f-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\nabla_{\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1},\alpha,\gamma_{k+1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}}f\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{k}}.

(89)

Given two charts, we will denote the “new” coordinates using Latin indices. We will use the symbol
\partial
to denote the basis vectors
\partial_{\gamma}
, to denote the Jacobian of the change of coordinates
\partial^{k}_{\gamma}
(so
\partial_{i}=\partial^{\gamma}_{i}\partial_{\gamma}
), and more generally
\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
will denote the operator consisting of partial differentiation according to the tuple
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
in the given chart
\varphi
, with
\partial^{\alpha}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\coloneqq\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\varphi^{\alpha}
. If
{\boldsymbol{k}}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})
, we will denote
\partial^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\partial^{k_{1}}_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{\gamma_{n}}
.

We are now ready to discuss the transfer principle. Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],M)
. We are looking for an expression in local coordinates

\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}=\frac{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|!}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}

(90)

for tuples
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, where we are summing over the tuple
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
,
S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
are locally-defined smooth functions on
M
and
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}
are the local expressions of the differentials of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
. As in the rest of the paper, in this section too we are only taking sums on tuples of length
\leq{\lfloor p\rfloor}
. (
90
) is meant to extend the usual differential of the simply bracketed
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
to the curved setting. When
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is a single index
\gamma
, we will denote
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}=\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\boldsymbol{X}^{\gamma}
, while keeping in mind that this still depends on the simple bracket. The first thing to notice is that
S
is only determined up to symmetry in the bottom indices, since it is being evaluated against
\mathrm{d}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}
. On the other hand, it could be the case that
S
is not symmetric in the top indices: this would mean that the
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}
’s may not be symmetric. Although this behaviour will be excluded in
Example 4.4
below, it cannot be excluded a priori.

We require two conditions of (
90
):

Itô-Kelly formula on manifolds.

For
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
the formula (
42
) holds with covariant differentiation replacing ordinary differentiation:

g(X)_{st}=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|!}\int_{s}^{t}\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}g(X)\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}.

(91)

Tensoriality.

\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
transforms like a contravariant tensor: the change of coordinates reads

\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{k}})}=\partial^{{\boldsymbol{k}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}.

(92)

While both requirements may seem to fall under the category change of variable formulae, the second cannot in general be inferred from the first, as is shown in
Example 4.4
below. It should also be remarked that, while for defining integration we will only need
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\boldsymbol{X}
to transform like a vector, the proof will show how tensoriality of the whole of
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
is indeed the more natural condition to require, and it would actually become a necessity when allowing more general integrals
\int f(\widetilde{X})\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
in
Theorem 4.3
below.

We proceed to write (
91
) by expanding its left hand side using the ordinary change of variable formula (
42
), and the right hand side by using the ansatz (
90
): we have

\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}g(X)S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}.

(93)

We may write
\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}g=\partial_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}gL^{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
, where the coefficients
L^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}
are determined up to symmetry in
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and can be computed in terms of the Christoffel symbols using (
89
).
L
can thus be seen as a map (dependent upon on the coordinate system)
\widetilde{T}^{{\lfloor p\rfloor}}(\mathbb R^{m})^{*}\to T^{{\lfloor p\rfloor}}(\mathbb R^{m})^{*}
, where
\widetilde{T}
denotes the symmetric tensor algebra, and
m
is the dimension of
M
. We will abbreviate these spaces
\widetilde{T}^{*}
,
T^{*}
(
\widetilde{T}
,
T
without dualisation).
L
is not graded, but is lower-diagonal (since the expression for
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g
only involves coordinate partial derivatives of
g
of order
\leq|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|
). Moreover, calling
\iota
the inclusion
\widetilde{T}\hookrightarrow T
,
\widetilde{L}\coloneqq\iota^{*}\circ L
is the identity on the diagonal (since the coefficient of
\partial_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}g
in the expression of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g
is
\text{\textdelta}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
up to symmetry, as can be seen from (
89
) by induction). These facts imply that
\widetilde{L}
is bijective, and thus
L
injective. We introduce the following convenient notation: round brackets above an equality means that the equality holds after symmetrising, i.e.

a_{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{n})}}{{=}}b_{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{\iff}}\quad\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}a_{\gamma_{\sigma(1)}\ldots\gamma_{\sigma(n)}}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}b_{\gamma_{\sigma(1)}\ldots\gamma_{\sigma(n)}}

(94)

where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
denotes the permutation group on
n
elements. Matching coefficients in (
93
) up to symmetry, and requiring the resulting identity to hold on the whole of
M
, we have

\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g=\partial_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}gL^{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}

(95)

for all
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Viewing
S
as a map
\widetilde{T}\to T
, this amounts to
S
being a right-inverse to
L^{*}
:

L^{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\boldsymbol{\beta}}),({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}}{{=}}\text{\textdelta}^{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}},\qquad\text{or}\quad L^{*}\circ S=\mathbbm 1_{\widetilde{T}}.

(96)

Here we have used the round bracket notation for symmetry (
94
). (
96
) is the natural condition on
S
that guarantees (
91
) for arbitrary elements of
\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],M)
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Of the many solutions, one stands out:

Definition 4.1

(Transfer symbols)
.

We define the
transfer symbols

\widetilde{\Gamma}
to be the unique solution
S
of (
96
) which is symmetric in the upper indices:
\widetilde{\Gamma}\coloneqq\iota\circ(L^{*}\circ\iota)^{-1}
, i.e. defined by the relation

\partial_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}g,\qquad g\in C^{\infty}(M)

(97)

with
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
symmetric in the upper indices (as well as in the lower ones).

We will often just write
\widetilde{\Gamma}
to denote the linear isomorphism
(L^{*}\circ\iota)^{-1}\colon\widetilde{T}\to\widetilde{T}
. The use of the symbol
\widetilde{\Gamma}
justified by the fact that
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}=\Gamma^{\gamma}_{(\alpha\beta)}
. This seems to suggest a possible connection between the transfer symbols with a single upper index and “higher-order Christoffel symbols”, which one may define by

\nabla_{\beta_{1}}\cdots\nabla_{\beta_{n-1}}\partial_{\beta_{n}}=\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}}\partial_{\alpha}.

(98)

For
n\geq 3
it is no longer true, however, that
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n}}=\Gamma^{\alpha}_{(\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{n})}
, as will be seen in
Example 4.4
below, and we do not see a more straightforward procedure to compute the
\widetilde{\Gamma}
’s, beyond that of inverting
L^{*}\circ\iota
.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
, defined with
S=\widetilde{\Gamma}
transforms like a tensor: at level
1
, this is what will allow us to integrate in a way that does not depend on the system of local coordinates. As done before, we seek a formulation of it that does not involve rough paths. Substituting in the ansatz (
90
) and applying the formula for pushforwards of simple brackets (
60
) to the right hand side

\displaystyle\frac{|{\boldsymbol{i}}|!}{|{\boldsymbol{j}}|!}S^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{j}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|!}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}\partial^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|!}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|!\cdots|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|!}\partial^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}\ldots{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m})}.

Since
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=|{\boldsymbol{i}}|
, the transformation law that must hold therefore is

S^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\boldsymbol{j}})}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}\ldots{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}={\boldsymbol{j}}\\
|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{|{\boldsymbol{j}}|!}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|!\cdots|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|!}\partial^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}}

(99)

where the
({\boldsymbol{j}})
is meant as a reminder that the identity is only considered up to symmetry in
{\boldsymbol{j}}
, which will be crucial in the next proof. Note, however, that since we are indexing
S
with tuples, we are summing over all non-empty tuples
{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}
whose concatenation equals
{\boldsymbol{j}}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

The transfer symbols
Definition 4.1
satisfy the change of coordinates (
99
).

The proof will proceed in three main steps:

1.

Reformulate (
99
) in a manner that takes symmetry of the tuple
{\boldsymbol{j}}
into account, i.e. as

S^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}=\partial^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}B^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}

with
B\colon\widetilde{T}\to\widetilde{T}
a function of the derivatives of the change of charts which is symmetric in
{\boldsymbol{j}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
. This means that for charts
\varphi
,
\psi
we have commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{\psi}\!}S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{\varphi}\!}S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

(100)

where
{{}^{\varphi}\!}S
,
{{}^{\psi}\!}S
are the map
S
in the two different charts and
\partial\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\lfloor p\rfloor}\partial^{\otimes n}
is the sum of tensor powers of the Jacobian of the change of coordinates.

2.

Derive a similar transformation rule
A\colon\widetilde{T}^{*}\to\widetilde{T}^{*}
for the (already known) coefficients
\widetilde{L}\coloneqq\iota^{*}\circ L
:

\widetilde{L}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}=A^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\widetilde{L}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\partial^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}

with
A
symmetric in
{\boldsymbol{i}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. This means commutative diagrams

(101)

3.

Finally, show the first identity in

B^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}A^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}}{{=}}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\text{\textdelta}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{2}}=\begin{cases}0&\text{for }|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|>0\\
\text{\textdelta}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{2}}&\text{for }|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|=0\end{cases}

(102)

with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{2}
,
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{2}|=|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|
. Moreover,
B^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}A^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=0
whenever
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|<|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|
. This will show
B=A^{*-1}
as maps
\widetilde{T}\to\widetilde{T}
.

Combining (
100
) and the dual of (
101
) we will have a commutative prism of bijections

\hbox to216.5pt{\vbox to112.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 108.25015pt\lower-56.08344pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-33.64734pt}{26.13423pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
