{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -116.07 -59.89 L -66.08 -32.85}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -116.07 -59.89 L -66.08 -32.85}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ .

The top and bottom faces commute by definition of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
and the two back faces commute by definitions of
A
and
B
, and by 3.: these facts imply that the front face also commutes, concluding the proof. Note that the same argument cannot be made to work if
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is replaced with a generic right inverse to
L^{*}
(and a counterexample to this effect is provided in
Example 4.4
below).

We begin with 1. Recall the notation for shuffles used earlier in
Theorem 2.10
:

\displaystyle B^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{j}}|!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{|{\boldsymbol{j}}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}\ldots{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}=\sigma_{*}{\boldsymbol{j}}\\
|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{|{\boldsymbol{j}}|!}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|!\cdots|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|!}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{j}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{j}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}}

since
\sigma^{-1}
can be written uniquely as a composition of a permutation that acts on the blocks
{\boldsymbol{j}}^{l}
(and there are
|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{1}|!\cdots|{\boldsymbol{j}}^{m}|!
of these) and a shuffle of them. Essentially the same formula is what yields the change of variable formula for the differential operator
\partial_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, as is easy to see by induction using the change of variable formula for a single partial derivative
\partial_{\gamma}=\partial^{k}_{\gamma}\partial_{k}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m!}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}}\partial_{k_{1}\ldots k_{m}}.

Recalling that
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f=\partial_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}fL^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, this implies

A^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{i}}|!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{i_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{i_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m}}

which gives 2. We now prove 3. It is clear that
B^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}A^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=0
for
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|<|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|
(since there are no terms to multiply), and (
102
) holds for
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|
, since in this case the only unshuffles considered in both cases are those in which the tuples
{\boldsymbol{k}}^{j}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{i}
have only one element (with
m!=n!
of both, each corresponding to a permutation), and the product reduces to

\sum_{\sigma,\rho\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\frac{1}{n!^{2}}\text{\textdelta}^{\sigma_{*}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{\rho_{*}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\frac{1}{n!}\text{\textdelta}^{\sigma_{*}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}.

We first show that in the case
|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|=m=1
(so that
B^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\partial^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
) it holds that (for
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|>0
)
B^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{k}}A^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\gamma{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=\partial_{\gamma}(B^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{k}}A^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\partial_{\gamma}\text{\textdelta}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=0
: we have (with a sum over
{\boldsymbol{k}}
, with
|{\boldsymbol{k}}|=n
)

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\bigg[\partial^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}\bigg]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\partial^{\beta}_{h{\boldsymbol{k}}}\partial^{h}_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}+\partial^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\\
l=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{l}}_{\gamma{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\partial^{\beta}_{h{\boldsymbol{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\\
l=0,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{l}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l}}\partial^{h}_{\gamma}\partial^{k_{l+1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l+1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}+\partial^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\\
l=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{l}}_{\gamma{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\partial^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\cdot\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}(\gamma{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}

where the last identity follows from distinguishing the case in which
\gamma
appears on its own in one of the unshuffled tuples of
\gamma{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
from the case in which it does not. Now let
|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|=m\geq 1
: we have, setting
|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{l}|\eqqcolon n_{l}

\displaystyle B^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}A^{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{k}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{k_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{k_{n}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}}\bigg)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{k}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}\bigg(\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{1}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{m}}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m}}\cdot\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1,1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152540}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152541}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}}}},\ldots\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m,1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}}}}}|>0\\
l=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}\partial^{k^{1}_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1,1}}\cdots\partial^{k^{1}_{n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152540}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152541}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}}}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{1,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{207628}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152540}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{152541}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}}}}}\cdots\cdots\partial^{k^{m}_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m,1}}\cdots\partial^{k^{m}_{n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{m,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336459}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle m$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{240560}{117965}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}}}}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{k}}^{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{k}}^{m}|>0\end{subarray}}{n\choose n_{1}\ldots n_{m}}^{-1}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\underbrace{\frac{1}{n_{l}!}\partial^{\beta_{l}}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{l}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}}}}})\in\mathrm{Sh}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\\
|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,1}|,\ldots,|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}}}}}|>0\end{subarray}}\partial^{k^{l}_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,1}}\cdots\partial^{k^{l}_{n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}}}}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{l,n_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{135988}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{90046}{196608}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}}}}}}}_{=0}.

Here we have split the second unshuffle in terms of the first one, in order to write the expression as a product of terms of the form treated previously. This concludes the proof of the three claims that were needed to establish the result.
∎

Rough integration can now be defined canonically. For simplicity, we will state it in the most important case of integrands given by one-forms, though it can be expected to hold for more general, appropriately defined controlled integrands with trace valued in
T^{*}M
. This more general definition of integrands (see
[
1
,
8
]
for how this theory is developed for non-geometric
[2,3)\ni p
-rough paths and for geometric rough paths of low regularity) would involve first defining the pullbacks of a controlled integrands, and then considering collections of controlled paths that agree under pullback via change of charts, i.e.
(\varphi_{\imath}\circ\varphi_{\jmath}^{-1})^{*}\,{{}^{\imath}\!}\boldsymbol{H}={{}^{\jmath}\!}\boldsymbol{H}
, as the objects to be integrated (examples would include, for instance, solutions to
T^{*}M
-valued RDEs). The main aspects of the theorem below, however, are already captured in the case in which we are integrating
f(X)
, for which no additional definitions are necessary.

Theorem 4.3

(Integration against branched rough paths on manifolds)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],M)
and
f\in\Gamma T^{*}M
. The expression

\int f(X)\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\boldsymbol{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}\int f_{\alpha}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}

(with sums on
\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
) does not depend on the system of local coordinates. Moreover, for
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

g(X)_{st}=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|!}\int_{s}^{t}\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}g(X)\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}

The integral between times
s
and
t
is defined by “patching”: pick a partition of the interval
s=u_{0}<u_{1}<\ldots<u_{n-1}<u_{n}=t
with the property that
X
stays in the same chart on each
[u_{i},u_{i+1}]
, and define
\int_{s}^{t}\!f(X)\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\boldsymbol{X}\coloneqq\sum_{i}\int_{u_{i}}^{u_{i+1}}\!f(X)\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\boldsymbol{X}
. By
Proposition 4.2
, this is independent of the choice of the chart chosen for each
i
, and it is independent of the choice of
\{u_{i}\}_{i}
since the integrals defined by two partitions must agree on their common refinement. When changing coordinates, associativity of integration
Proposition 2.6
is necessary when integrating against the transformed rough path, and can be applied by viewing Kelly’s change of variable formula as an RDE driven by
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
. The Itô formula holds by definition of the transfer symbols
Definition 4.1
.
∎

Example 4.4

(Transfer symbols of order
\leq 3
)
.

We find a general solution of (
96
) up to level
3
, which reads

\partial_{\alpha\beta\gamma}g=\nabla_{\lambda}gS^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+\nabla_{\mu\nu}gS^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+\nabla_{\lambda\mu\nu}gS^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}.

(103)

For
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
we compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{\gamma}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{\gamma}g

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha\beta}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha\beta}g-\partial_{\delta}g\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha\beta\gamma}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha\beta\gamma}g-\partial_{\delta}g\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\beta\gamma}-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\gamma\delta}g-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\gamma}\partial_{\beta\delta}g-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\alpha\delta}g+\partial_{\varepsilon}g\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\delta\gamma}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\beta}+\partial_{\varepsilon}g\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\beta\delta}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\gamma}.

The presence of the term
\partial_{\alpha\beta\gamma}g
in the expression for
\nabla_{\alpha\beta\gamma}g
implies

\displaystyle S^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{6}}\epsilon_{\sigma}\text{\textdelta}^{\sigma_{*}(\lambda\mu\nu)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\qquad\qquad\text{with }\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{6}}\epsilon_{\sigma}=1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{6}}\epsilon_{\sigma}\text{\textdelta}^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\sigma_{*}(\alpha\beta\gamma)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\alpha\beta\gamma)}}{{=}}\text{\textdelta}^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}.

Substituting into (
103
) we get

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}gS^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}+(\partial_{\mu\nu}g-\partial_{\lambda}g\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu})S^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle-\partial_{\delta}g\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\beta\gamma}-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\delta\gamma}g-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\gamma}\partial_{\beta\delta}g-\Gamma^{\delta}_{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\alpha\delta}g+\partial_{\varepsilon}g\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\delta\gamma}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\beta}+\partial_{\varepsilon}g\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\beta\delta}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\alpha\gamma}=0.

Setting the sum of all the second derivatives to zero yields

S^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mu\nu),(\alpha\beta\gamma)}}{{=}}3\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\text{\textdelta}^{\nu}_{\gamma},

where the symmetrisation in
\mu,\nu
comes from the fact that in the previous expression this was multiplied by
\partial_{\mu\nu}g
. The general parametric expression for
S^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
, not necessarily symmetric in the upper indices, is thus given by

S^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\alpha\beta\gamma)}}{{=}}c\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\text{\textdelta}^{\nu}_{\gamma}+(3-c)\Gamma^{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\text{\textdelta}^{\mu}_{\gamma},\qquad c\in\mathbb R.

Re-substituting, we can solve for
S^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
. The general solution up to symmetry in the bottom indices
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}S^{\lambda}_{\gamma}&&S^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}&&S^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\\
\\
S^{\mu\nu}_{\gamma}&&S^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}&&S^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\\
\\
S^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\gamma}&&S^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}&&S^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\text{\textdelta}^{\lambda}_{\gamma}&&\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}&&\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\beta\gamma}+\Gamma^{\lambda}_{(\gamma\sigma)}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}+(3-2c)\Gamma^{\lambda}_{[\gamma\sigma]}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\\
\\
0&&\text{\textdelta}^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}&&c\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\text{\textdelta}^{\nu}_{\gamma}+(3-c)\Gamma^{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\text{\textdelta}^{\mu}_{\gamma}\\
\\
0&&0&&\text{\textdelta}^{\lambda\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\end{pmatrix},\qquad c\in\mathbb R.

where the square brackets denote antisymmetrisation. The value of
c
that makes the third element on the second row symmetric is
c=3/2
: substituting this value yields the transfer symbols needed for the transfer principle when
p<4
. In particular

\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\alpha\beta\gamma)}}{{=}}\partial_{\gamma}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}+\Gamma^{\lambda}_{(\gamma\sigma)}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}.

(104)

The expression for a rough integral against a manifold-valued
[3,4)\ni p
-branched rough path
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
is therefore

\int f(X)\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\boldsymbol{X}=\int f_{\lambda}(X)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}f_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}+\frac{1}{6}f_{\lambda}\big(\partial_{\gamma}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}+\Gamma^{\lambda}_{(\gamma\sigma)}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\big)\hskip-1.00006pt(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}.

(105)

We may now ask the question of whether other values of
c
result in the correct transformation rule being satisfied. Keeping in mind the well-known transformation rule for Christoffel symbols

\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=\partial^{k}_{\gamma}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{j}+\partial^{k}_{\gamma}\partial^{\gamma}_{ij}

(106)

and that torsion is a tensor, the change of basis rule for the additional term is given by

\Gamma^{l}_{[hk]}\Gamma^{h}_{ij}=\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{[\sigma\gamma]}\partial^{\sigma}_{h}\partial^{\gamma}_{k}(\partial^{h}_{\tau}\Gamma^{\tau}_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{j}+\partial^{h}_{\delta}\partial^{\delta}_{ij})=\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{[\sigma\gamma]}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{j}\partial^{\gamma}_{k}+\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{[\sigma\gamma]}\partial^{\sigma}_{ij}\partial^{\gamma}_{k}.

On the other hand, by
Proposition 4.2

\widetilde{\Gamma}^{l}_{ijk}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ijk)}}{{=}}\partial_{\lambda}^{l}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{j}\partial^{\gamma}_{k}+2\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{(\alpha\beta)}\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{jk}+\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\partial^{\lambda}_{ijk}

Therefore, calling
S(c)
the solution of (
103
) with parameter
c
, we have

\displaystyle S^{l}_{ijk}(c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ijk)}}{{=}}\partial^{l}_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}(c)\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{j}\partial^{\gamma}_{k}+2\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{(\alpha\beta)}\partial^{\alpha}_{i}\partial^{\beta}_{jk}+\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\partial^{\lambda}_{ijk}+(3-2c)\partial^{l}_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{[\sigma\gamma]}\partial^{\sigma}_{ij}\partial^{\gamma}_{k}

which is not the correct transformation rule in general unless
c=3/2
, because of the presence of the last term. This includes
c=1
, in which case we obtain the solution (
98
) and
S^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\partial_{\gamma}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}+\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\gamma\sigma}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}
: the symmetrisation of
\gamma
and
\sigma
in (
104
) is therefore crucial. Of course, if
\nabla
is torsion-free, then
S(c)=\widetilde{\Gamma}
for all
c\in\mathbb R
, but at higher orders one can expect curvature and its derivatives also to generate similar obstructions to the transformation rule being the required one, even if torsion vanishes.

This shows that (at least insofar as the symbols with
3
indices or fewer are concerned) the only choice for
S
that results in the two conditions (
91
) and (
92
) being satisfied (uniformly over all
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
,
M
,
\nabla
and
g
) is given by the transfer symbols
Definition 4.1
.

5
Quasi-geometric RDEs on manifolds

In this section we will show how the transfer principle
Definition 4.1
can also be used to give canonical meaning to equations of the form

\mathrm{d}_{N}Y=F(Y,X)\mathrm{d}_{M}\boldsymbol{X},\quad Y_{0}=y_{0}

(107)

in the case in which
\boldsymbol{X}
is quasi-geometric: by this we mean that each
{{}^{\imath}\!}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
is quasi-geometric; note that proving that pushforwards of quasi-geometric rough paths are quasi-geometric would allows us to only require this on a specific atlas.
M
and
N
are smooth manifolds (
y_{0}
a point in
N
),
F
is a field of linear maps from
TM
to
TN
, i.e. an element of the space of sections
\Gamma\mathcal{L}(TM,TN)
, where
\mathcal{L}(TM,TN)
is viewed as a vector bundle over
N\times M
(whose fibre at the point
(y,x)
is the space of all linear maps
T_{x}M\to T_{y}N
). This is the setting adopted in
[
13
]
; the benefit of allowing both driver and solution to be manifold value can be seen, for example, when defining parallel transport above
X
, which can be viewed as satisfying an RDE valued in a fibre bundle above
\boldsymbol{X}
.
M
and
N
are endowed with covariant derivatives, and the differentials
\mathrm{d}_{M}
,
\mathrm{d}_{N}
indicate the use of the transfer principle w.r.t. each connection. Unravelling the definition, we can write this as

\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{j}}|!}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{i}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{j}})}=\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}F^{i}_{\alpha}(Y,X)\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}.

(108)

Here Greek indices are used on
M
and Latin ones on
N
; this convention is also relied upon for distinguishing the two sets of symbols
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, which refer to two separate connections. By (
108
) we mean that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Y}}
is a simple bracket extension and equality holds after integrating both sides, by patching, in arbitrary charts: the expression is invariant under choice of a partition used for integrating and change of local coordinates both on
M
and on
N
, again by
Proposition 4.2
(and
Proposition 2.6
). This does not require quasi-geometricity of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
, but is useless on its own, since (
108
) does not bear the form (
76
), since bracket terms of the solution
Y
appear on the left hand side. This means we may not appeal to well-known existence and uniqueness results
[
26
, §3.2]
, and there is no guarantee that a solution which simultaneously satisfies the bracket constraints exists. The main challenge is to show how (
108
) is equivalent to an equation written in local coordinates as

\mathrm{d}Y^{k}={{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{k}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(Y,X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}

(109)

with the coefficients
{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F
are defined in terms of
F
and the two connections. Note that “signal-dependent” RDEs, i.e. ones of the form
\mathrm{d}Y=G(Y,X)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}
can always be recast as ones in the standard form (
28
) by viewing
X
as part of the solution. Signal dependence is necessary when
X
is manifold-valued (as already noticed in
[
13
]
for SDEs), since the tangent spaces of
M
are not all canonically identified (and in fact cannot be unless
M
is parallelisable), meaning
F
in (
107
) must be allowed to depend on
X
.

To see how such a formula can be obtained, it is helpful to start with the case in which
3\leq p<4
(see
[
1
, (2.29)]
for the
2\leq p<3
case, already derived in
[
14
, p.428]
in the semimartingale case). For simplicity, we also assume
M=\mathbb R^{m}
is Euclidean space and
F
does not depend on
X
(which is made possible by the previous assumption, since the tangent spaces
T_{x}\mathbb R^{m}
for
x\in\mathbb R^{m}
can all be canonically identified with
\mathbb R^{m}
): (
108
) then reads

\mathrm{d}Y^{k}+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{k}_{ij}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(ij)}+\frac{1}{6}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{k}_{ijh}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(ijh)}=F^{k}_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{\gamma}.

(110)

Since the second and third summands on the left will only contribute
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
terms of order
2
and
3
, we know that the coefficient of order
1
of this equation, written in the form (
109
), is
F^{k}_{\gamma}
. The rule for simple brackets of quasi-geometric RDEs
Example 2.9
implies that we can write

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(ij)}=F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}+\ldots

with the dots (from now on) denoting terms of order
3
. In turn, resubstituting into (
110
), means we can write

\mathrm{d}Y^{k}=F^{k}_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{k}_{ij}F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}+\ldots

We can now use this to calculate
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(ij)}
precisely up to order
3
:

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(ij)}=F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}-\frac{1}{2}\big(\Gamma^{i}_{hl}F^{h}_{\alpha}F^{l}_{\gamma}F^{j}_{\beta}+F^{i}_{\alpha}\Gamma^{j}_{hl}F^{h}_{\beta}F^{l}_{\gamma}\big)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}.

Considering that
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(ijk)}=F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}F^{k}_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}
by the same
Theorem 3.4
, and using the expressions for the transfer symbols in terms of the Christoffel symbols
Example 4.4
, we have, substituting everything back into (
110
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}Y^{k}={}

\displaystyle F^{k}_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{k}_{(ij)}F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\frac{1}{4}\Gamma^{k}_{(ij)}\big(\Gamma^{i}_{hl}F^{h}_{\alpha}F^{l}_{\gamma}F^{j}_{\beta}+F^{i}_{\alpha}\Gamma^{j}_{hl}F^{h}_{\beta}F^{l}_{\gamma}\big)-\frac{1}{6}\big(\partial_{i}\Gamma^{k}_{jh}+\Gamma^{k}_{(hl)}\Gamma^{l}_{ij}\big)F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}F^{k}_{\gamma}\bigg]\!(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}.

Simplifying, we obtain:

Example 5.1

(Quasi-geometric RDEs on manifolds,
3\leq p<4
)
.

(
110
) can equivalently be rewritten in the form of (
109
) as

\mathrm{d}Y^{k}=F^{k}_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta)}+\bigg[\frac{1}{3}\Gamma^{k}_{(hl)}\Gamma^{l}_{ij}-\frac{1}{6}\partial_{i}\Gamma^{k}_{jh}\bigg]\!(Y)F^{i}_{\alpha}F^{j}_{\beta}F^{h}_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{(\alpha\beta\gamma)}.

(111)

It is possible to check, using
Corollary 3.3
(which has not been used in its derivation) that the expression is invariant under change of
i,j,k,h
-coordinates (not
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
-coordinates, which we have fixed).

The method used to derive this equation extends to the general case. In the following theorem, we will write
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n)}}{{=}}
to mean equality that is accurate at order
n
in the bracket
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
(i.e. the difference only consists of bracket terms of orders
n+1
and above).

Theorem 5.2

(Quasi-geometric RDEs on manifolds)
.

The rough integral relation (
108
), which is invariant under changes of coordinates on
M
and
N
, is equivalent to an equation of the form (
109
) with the coefficients
{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F
determined by the following recursive procedure: first set
{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{k}_{\gamma}\coloneqq F^{k}_{\gamma}
(
\gamma
a single index) and then, beginning with
n=2
, perform step 1. below, perform step 2., increment
n
and repeat. The coefficients will be fully determined once step 2. with
n={\lfloor p\rfloor}
is reached.

1.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(i_{1}\ldots i_{m})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n)}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{i}|\geq 1\\
|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}|+\ldots+|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}|\leq n\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}|!\cdots|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m}|!}{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{i_{1}}_{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1})}\cdots{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{i_{m}}_{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m})}(Y,X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{1}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{m})}
;

2.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}Y^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|!}F^{k}_{\alpha}(Y,X)\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}(X)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}{}^{({\boldsymbol{\beta}})}-\sum_{m=2}^{n}\frac{1}{m!}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{k}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}}(Y)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}^{(i_{1}\ldots i_{m})}
.

We explain how (
108
) implies the recursion, and how the recursion determines the coefficients.

•

Step 1. is an application of
Theorem 3.4
; it is crucial to note that this only requires
{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{k}_{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}
for
|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|\leq n-1
, which are known, considering that step 2. has just been executed at level
n-1
, or in the case of
n=2
thanks to the base case.

•

Step 2. expresses
\mathrm{d}Y^{k}
in the desired form, accurately at order
n
, and is implied by the fact that bracket terms
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}
of order
\geq n+1
, by
Theorem 3.4
, do not contribute bracket terms
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
of order
\leq n
(and the same reasoning holds for the base case
{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{k}_{\gamma}\coloneqq F^{k}_{\gamma}
). It is obtained by substituting in the original equation (
108
) the expressions for
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}{}^{(i_{1}\ldots i_{m})}
in terms of
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}
, for
2\leq m\leq n
and accurate at order
n
, which were obtained recursively, since step 1. has just been performed with the same
n
. This determines
{{}^{\nabla}\hskip-3.00003pt}F^{k}_{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}})}
for
|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}|\leq n
.

The final form of the equation is reached once step 2. is executed at level
{\lfloor p\rfloor}
, since there are no bracket terms of higher order. Conversely, that the resulting equation of the form (
109
) implies the original (
108
) is immediately implied by 2. at order
{\lfloor p\rfloor}
, considering that the expression for the terms
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{Y}
that it yields is consistent with those already derived at previous steps. This concludes the proof.
∎

6
Conclusions and further directions

With this article, we hope to have convinced the reader that there is a canonical way of integrating against branched rough paths on manifolds and, in the quasi-geometric case, of understanding their manifold-valued RDE solutions.

The main way to extend this work would be to write a transfer principle that works for RDEs driven by general branched rough paths. As discussed in
Remark 2.3
, even full bracket extensions are not closed under lifts of their controlled paths. For this reason, it appears that such a transfer principle would have to depend on an iterated kind of bracket extension.

It would also be interesting to derive extrinsic formulae for integrals and RDEs based on transfer principles, i.e. in terms of ambient coordinates when
M
and
N
are embedded. This is done for
2\leq p<3
in
[
1
, Ch. 3]
. While local coordinates have the advantage of not requiring an embedding, formulae in ambient coordinates generally have more satisfying geometric interpretations, e.g. in terms of tangent and normal directions of the correction terms.

Recall from (
70
) that there is a canonical isomorphism
\exp\colon T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\shuffle}\to T(\widetilde{\mathbb}R^{d})_{\widetilde{\shuffle}}
with inverse
\log
. After checking that this map commutes with pushforwards via smooth maps, i.e.
f_{*}\exp^{*}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}})=\exp^{*}(f_{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}})
, and that thus (letting
f
be the change of charts)
\exp^{*}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}})\eqqcolon\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}
is well-defined for quasi-geometric
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\widetilde{C}^{p}_{\omega}([0,T],M)
, it is very natural to hope for the RDE (
107
) to be equivalent to one driven by the geometric rough path
\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}\coloneqq\exp^{*}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{X}})
of the form
\mathrm{d}Y=F(Y,X)\mathrm{d}\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}+\ldots
, where the dots denote correction terms generated by
\log^{*}
and involving the connection
\nabla
(which is no longer involved in integration itself, since the meaning of equations driven by geometric rough paths is independent of the connection). To state and prove such a result, a better understanding of the Davie expansion for quasi-geometric RDEs on manifolds is necessary.

In
[
9
]
the authors use the Munthe-Kaas Wright Hopf algebra of planar rooted trees
[
39
]
to define a special kind of differential equation: given a Lie group
G
, with associated Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=T_{1_{G}}G
, a smooth manifold
M
acted on transitively by
G
, smooth maps
F_{\gamma}\colon M\to\mathfrak{g}
, and defining vector fields
\#F_{\gamma}\in\Gamma TM
by

\#F_{\gamma}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigg|_{0}\exp(tF_{\gamma}(x)).x

where
\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\to G
is the Lie-algebraic exponential map, they give meaning to

\mathrm{d}Y=\#F_{\gamma}(Y)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{\gamma}

where
\boldsymbol{X}
is an
\mathbb R^{d}
-valued
planarly branched
rough path. It would be interesting, especially once our transfer principle is extended to RDEs as detailed above, to compare our definition of RDE with theirs, and in particular to see whether/how the additional data of the connection needed in our case corresponds to the planar structure required in theirs.
